INTRODUCTION. 


Tue greatest difficulty in connection with this plan has been to 
work the forests in such a way as not to unduly interfere with the privi- 
leges of the villagers, and I may say that but for these privileges the 
whole programme of work would be greatly changed and forest produce 
could be delivered at Chakrata at cheaper rates. 

Tn addition to the ordinary work connected with the preparation of 
a plan of this kind, all the cultivation in the 2nd-class forests had 
to be demarcated and chaks formed, whilst an enumeration of all the 
cattle, sheep and goats which graze in the forests was also made. 


The following reports, &c., were of great service in the preparation 
of the plan :— 

Preliminary Working Plan of the Deoban Woiking Circle, 1875-76 to 1877-78, 

by Sir D Brandis 

Suggestions by Sir D Brandis, 1879 and 1881. 

Settlement Reports of Jauns4r-Bawar. 

Forest Conference Report, 1875, with paper on nghts in Jaunsér, by Mr. Bagshawe. 

Statement of forest mghts in Jaunsfr, by Colonel Bailey, R.E 

Specification, Deoban sledge road, by Mr. Hobart-Hampden, 

Note on the geology of Jaunsér by Mr R. D, Oldham, 

Gazetteer, North-Western Provinces. 

The Indian Forester 

Annual Reports on Forest Administration, North-Western Provinces and Oudh, 

Plantation Notes by forest students, 

Office records. 

I desire to express my thanks to Messrs Moir and Hobart-Iampden, 
the two officers in charge of the Division during the preparation of this 
plan, for the ready assistance they have given on every occasion. 


T also wish to express my great pleasure at the energy and intelli- 
gence displayed by Forest Ranger Keshavanand whilst assisting me in 
this work. He has described all the compartments in the range and has 
prepared an excellent stock map, besides measuring the heights of numer- 
ous trees and making a good collection of rock specimens and plants.

WORKING PLAN OF THE DEOBAN RANGE, 
JAUNSAR FOREST DIVISION, 
N..W. PROVINCES AND OUDH. 


By N. HEARLE, 
Deputy Capservator of Forests, Sohool Cirole, 


CHAPTER I. 


Situation, AREA, BoUNDARIES AND CLIMATE, 


1. THE main object of the present working plan is to provide a permanent sup- 
ply of fuel and timber for the cantonmente of Chakréta, and consequently all forests 
within workable distance (about 10 miles for fuel and 13 miles for timber) have been 
included, as well as the Kalsi forest at the foot of the Himdlaya, 27 miles from can- 
tonments, but connected with them by a fine cart-road. These forests, which include 
the whole of the Deoban range, consist of-= 


Acres 
1 The Deoban forests to the north of and above Chakréta a. §=24,018 
2 Tho amall detached onk forests south of Chakrita = 775

The cantonment forests are all included within the cantonment boundary, which 
is well defined by a cut line. 


6. Changes in tha outer boundary,—Many changes have been made in the onter 
bowndary in connection with the preparation of this working plan usually in 
order to exclude patches of cultivation; but the largest area has been abandoned 
in the Chitri block in order to open out the large village of Koti, which was 
previously surrounded by Government forests. Altogether 887 acres have béen 
excluded from the aren under foreat management, consisting mostly of grazing lands 
or of ground containing scattered plots of cultivation. All these changes are shown 


iv a tabular form in Appendix F. 


a 


7. The small detached forest of Mogad, covering 117 acre ia khats Bamtér 
and Dissau, was considered not worth retaining, and by G. O. No. a dated the 
let July, 1886, it was constituted a village forest, the first in these Provinces; the 
azea of the forest tv khat Bamtér being assigued to the village of Lurli and that in 


khat Dissau to the village of Haja. 


8. Climate.—Full particulars concerning the climate of these hills in general 
has been giveu by Mr. Fernandez iu his working plan of the Mund4li forest of this 
Division, to which reference is invited. It is only necessary to say here that the 
statement made in paragraph 28 of that plan, to the effect that the frost which occurs 
in October and November is not dangerous to vegetable life, appears to require modifi- 
cation. It has becn found necessary to protect young deodér and other seedlings 
growing in nurseries from its effect by means of shades formed of ringéls.and grass 
raised two or three feet from the ground, and the neglect of this precaution last year 
in the nursery close to the Bodiér bungalow waa, doubtless, the reason why so urany 
of the seedlings died. It was fouud that the frost was eo severe in the valleys and 
hollows at Bodidr that uurseries which were formed there had to be abaudoned.

12, The Indian Forest Act appeared in 1878, and it haviag been then assumed 
that the enquiry prescribed in section 94 of the Act had been made, the lat and 
2nd class Government forests wore gazetted as “ reserved” under that section (see 
G. O. No. 408A., dated Ist August, 1878). 


13. In 1881 the khat boundaries withia lat and 2nd class forests were entered 
onthe map and have been cheoked by Mr. Litchfield im the doubtful eases; it is 
believed that they ale now quite accurate. 


14. Provision for closing 2nd-clase forest.—Should the Forest Officer at any 
time wish to close any portion of 2ud-elass forest and make it let class, ho must 
give a month’s notice through the Superintendent of the Dun and leave the villagers 
sufficient forest for their yse. If they deem the forest so left insufficient, or that 
remote forests only have been left open, they can apply to the Superintendent to get 
just arrangements made for grazing, &c. So long as such 2nd-class forests are 
closed, they can be treated precisely as Ist-class forests; but they differ from the 
latter in this respeot, that they are only closed temporarily to admit of reproduction. 
As soon as they are sufficiently grown up to admit of their being grazed over without 
risk, they will be opened and other areas of 2nd-class forest will be closed. 


15. Limitation of privileges.—In the new settlement of 1838-84 by Mr. Ross, 
arrangements were made by which the forcst rules, where they pressed hardly, were 
relaxed and provision was made for the requirements at Chakréta for fuel and 
proper forest conservancy, withoyt yndue or unnecessary restrictions of the privileges 
of the villagers. With the exception of buildiug timber and of special cases, where it 
has been recorded that the inhabitants of one khat have the right to graze their 
flocks in a forest which originally belonged to some other khat, these privileges only 
exist in each khat in that portion of the 2nd-class forests which was originally 
contained within the boundary of that kbat, but within that portion all villagera 
of the khat possess rights. The privileges of grazing, cutting leaves and grass for 
fodder, collecting leaves for manure and wood for fuel, are still nearly unlimited ; 
but if their gradual curtailment and definition be kept in view, it is hoped they will 
be oxercised with the least possible damage to the forest growth. Mr. Ross, the 
Superintendent of the Dan,and Mr. Cornwall, the Settlement Officer in 1873, carefully 
provided in the record-of-rights for the gradual definitjon of all rights in the forest. 


The different forest privileges.—Tho different forest privileges will now be treated 
of in detail. 


16. (1) Timber.—Bofore 1870, the people both of the hills and the neighbour- 
jog gountry below were in the habit of cutting and carrying away deoddr and what- 
ever wood they pleased to a very large extent, and some of the foresta, as Mashak 
and Bodiér, were very heavily worked for this purpose, to the great profit of the 
neighbouring zamindérs, as those who came from below had to pay something 
(though it was a trifle) to the siy4na and to the zamindérsa on whose lands they cut 
the wood. .

The Jaunséris are very particular about their houses: they all have dauble- 
storied houses, and in many instances three and four sturies. In the lower khate 
the wood work is deodar procured from the higher khats, but the covering is alate ; 
in the upper khata the roof 1s usually deadér, split planks being used. In the upper 
khats, people say, there is no slate, but the reason probably often ia that it is easier 
for the people to split deodér than to quarry alate, for which hitherto, awing to the 
ample supply of deodér, there has been no incentive to look. 


17, (2) Fuel. —The villagers have the right of taking dry wood for fuel for their 
own use from the 2nd-clags forents, 


‘18. (3) Grazing.—~It has.been mentioned that the villagara have a general 
right of grazing in the 2nd-cluss forests of their khats, and in certain specified 
instances in similar forests of other khats, but that the foresp officer, with the sanc- 
tion of the Superintendent of the Dén, has the power of closing some of thoso forests 
until reproduction is ensured. Connected with this is the burning of grassy slopes 
within the forest area which has been placed under the oontrol of tha Forest Depart- 
ment. This control gives the forest officer considerable influence with the villagers 
who are always allowed to burn such slopes where no damage ean be dane ta the 
forest, whenever they ask permission, provided they have heen observant of the 
forest rules, The villagers are responsible for any fire which may occur in 2nd- 
glass forests protected from fire, but open to grazing, and should a fire take place 
and they caupot prove that it was caused by villagers from another khat, they are 
liable te a fiva

19. (4) Lopping.—In 2nd-class forests the villagers can eut leaves for their 
sheep with the permission of the forest officer. As this loppmg is only, therefore, 
allowed with the permission of the forest officer, that officer can clearly make rules 
for its regulation. In fact, in the wajib-ul-arz of all the khats except Barasuwa, 
Birmau, Udpfita, Semalta, Seligothan, and Haripur Bids, it is expressly laid down 
that no branches are to be cut over one inch in diameter and that no trees under 
15 feet in height shall be lopped. 


20. (5) Removing dead leaves.—In 2nd-class forests the villagers have the 
right of collecting fallen leaves under the trees for manure. 


21, (6) Building stone.—The villagers possess tke right of quarrying in 2nd- 
class forests. ‘ 


22. (7) Ringdls. —They have the right of cutting ringéls for their own use 
in 2nd-class forests and of selling them from the forests of the drd-class, 


23. (8) Treee for hedging purposes.— With the permission of the forest officer 
they can cut trees such as ginghar (Crategus Pyracantha), kaith, lod (Symplocoa ep,), 
ampar and mekh for hedging purposes. 


24. (9) Cultivation.—In 1st-class forests all cultivated land was taken up 
under the Land Acquisition Act and full compevsation paid to the villagers. In 
pearly all 2nd-clase forests there are numerous cultivated patches which the 
villagers have a right to cultivate if sugh fields are entered in the assessment lists, 
and amargin of 20 feet is allowed around such fields where the owner hasa right 
to lop trees which overshadow his crops. If any zamindfr wishes to break up 
new land in # 2nd-class forest within his khat, he must, before doing so, obtain 


( 9 ) 


the permission of the forest officer as regards the quantity of land, the position of 
the field, and the period for which it is to be cultivated ; and he shall also pay 
such revenue as may be fixed by the Superimtendent of the Dun. After the 
expiry of this term he must get fresh permission; if he does not get it he muat 
rolinquish the field, and uuder no circumstances can he acquire any right whatever 


in it. 
25. (10) Rights to wafer and rights of way.—The villagers possess the snme 


right over the water which flows through the 2nd-class foresta as they have 
hitherto enjoyed, and the rights of way to such water also reinains iutact. 


26. (11) Fishing.—The villagers have the right to fish three times a year by 
poisoning and throughout the year by rod and line; they can also fish with nets in 
the larger rivers as the Jumna, Tons, and Amlawa, but not in the small streams.

28 Stepa taken by the Forest Department ta provide for and to regulate the various

have been tuken by the Forest Department to provide for and to regulate them. 


(1) The rights to free-grant timber cannot accumulate nor can the wood be 
sold. No zamindér can sell any wood or tree of any kind to any person, such as 
contractor or inhabitant of Chakrata, or to the servants of Public Works or Commis- 
sariat Department or any other outsider. The zam{ind4rs cannot even sell amongst 
themselves such trees as are self-grown, but they cau give them to people of another 
khat for nothing, or in lieu of sometbing received from them. The sale of trees 
planted by the vendors is permissible to inhabitants of the pargana. The proposals 
contained in the joint memorandum on forest rights in Jaunsér Bawar by Mr, 
Ross, O.S. Mr. Baker, O.S., and Mr. Fisher, Officrating Conservator of Forests, 
dated January, 1885 (Appendix G), were sanctioned by G. QO. No. 2" of 15th

The orders contained in paragruph 3 regarding the use of the saw instead of the 
axe caused, as anticipated, a good deal of dissatisfaction amongst certain of the 
more backward and remote khats, and in consequence only 18 out of the 39 khats 
removed their timber during 1885-86. The enforcement of this rule bas since been 
deferred for two years ia order to allow the villagers time to adapt thomeelves to 
the change. Even, however, when the axe was allowed, the quantity of timber 
removed was frequently ynder the amount to which the people were entitled. The 
cause of this may be that the quantity entered in the agreements is greater than 
that which the people really require, or it may be duein any particular year toa 
scarcity of food which renders it difficult for them to remove the produce.

( 13 ) 


that in future no further area should be closed, unl]: ss a corresponding area is opened 
in which reproductiou has been effected and iu which the young growth is no longer

Din should be obtained, and any cnse of difference of opinion between the Conser- 
vator of Forests and the Superintendent of the |)an should be referred for decision 
to Government. Mr. Rosa in hia late settlement report says: “ The following are the 
khats close to Chakrita from which, in my opinion, no more grazing can be taken:— 
Dhananu, Misséu, Mohna, Bumtar, and Dwar. In neither Mohna nor Bamtér ie there 
any more forest which under agy circumstances could be closed, but in Dhanau and 
Miss4u there is there a good deal of forest which could be closed if a corresponding

might be mada, but vot much. Kailo is the next nearest khat to Chakrata; from it 
a guod deal of land has been takeu already, and it is hampered by having Ist-claes 
forest close to its principal village, but still I think it possible that more forest 
could be taken up in it. I think the Forest, Department should havo no difficulty, by 
judiciously re-opening forests where possible, in closing at one time or another every 
acre of forest near Chakrfta.” The closing affwots privileges Nos. (2), (8), (4), (5), (7) 
and 8) of paras. 16-26, but cultivation is not interfered with. An excellent plan bas 
been adopted for marking off the open and closad areas on the ground, the boundaries 
between the two portions beiny indicated by posts bearing boards colored red towards 
the closed and black towards the open side. Such posts serve asa guide to shepherds 
and others. 

80. /rivilege No. (4), Lopping.— This has hitherto been permitted by the forest 
officer in all upen 2nd-class forcats; but Mr. Smythies established a rule by which 
no trees under four feet in girth were to be lopped and that no branches over six 
inches in girth were to be cut. Inu 1885 Mr. Moir, the forest officer in charge, in 
cousultation with Mr. Rogs, prepared the following rules :— 

(1) No trée under three inches if girth or one inch in diameter shall be lopped. 

(2) No tranches or twigs over three inches in girth or one inch in diameter 
to be cut. 

(3) Trees shall only be Jopped once in three years, 

At Kurwa, the villagers are now allowed to remove al] the small twiga from the 

trees felled for coppicing, and as the area coppiced is annually about three acres, this 
affurds a considerable quantity of fudder for winter use; but they have abused the 
privilege by lopping some of the stasdurds left in the coppice, 
“ 31 Privilege No. (5\, Removing dead levves—This is not permitted in closed 
2ud-class forests except at Kurwa, where annual permission, which can of course 
be withdrawn, is given to remove leaves from certain of the coppice compartments; 
and Mr. Moir suggests that it should be restricted there to 300 ‘ kaudies’ full per 
anpum, or that half the forest should be opened only each alternate year. 


32. Privilege ‘No. (9), Cultivation.—Since the demarcation, owing to the difficulty 
of supervision, aud perhaps also to venality on the part of forest guards, numerous 
fresh patches of cultivation have been made without sanction, and no steps were 
taken to effectually check this inveterate custom of hill races until this working 
plan was begun. All cultivation assessed to revenue in the forests of the range bas 
been visited by me, the patwaris being present with their maps. Where possible, 
the outer boundary was altered so as to exclude such cultivation; but where this 
was not feasible, a ‘chak’ was formed by meaus of pillars, which will be connected 
with each other, where the boundary is an artificial one, by moans of cut lines. It 
was necessary of course, owing to the scattered position of the eultivated plots, to 
gacrifice pieces of forest in order to forin a workable boundary; so that altogether 
these chaks occupy 712 acres, and all of them have been entered on the maps, A 


list of these chaks is given in Appendix L, 
4D 


(' 14 ) 


In some inatancos it is proposed to make exchanges or to purchase fields, and 
these unsettled cases are discussed in paragraph 190 of Chapter IX, ‘The Working 
Plan.’ 


All the chak pillars near Konain dnd Banifna have been built, but the building 
of those about Deoban and in Lokandi bas been delayed for want of lime, This is 
now ready und the work will soon be finished. The Rangor has been ordered to 
peg out the’cut Jines wherever necessary in places where they have not yet been dug.

35. The Deoban forests,—Thea forests included in the Deoban range to the north 
of Chakrita are divided into two parts bya prominent ridge which forms tho water- 
shed line between the Jumna and Tons. This ridge, after rising from the juno- 
tion of the two rivers, enters these forests at piMar No. 7 in the Darna block, where 
it has an altitude of about 7,700 feet. Thenoé it rises rather abruptly to Chakrata 
View, 9,331 feet, running from this pvint in a north-easterly direction, the altitude 
varying only a few hundred feet up to the Karémba peak, 10,075 feet, the highest 
point in Jaunsfr and also the eastern limit of the range. The slopes on the northern 
flank of the ridge occupy most of the wooded parts of the upper basins of the Bin4l 
g4d with its large tributaries, the Shili, Mashak, and Ghérawah g&da, the Amtifr gid, 
the-Jumréd gad, and taro smaller tributaries of the Tons in the extreme west, whilst 
those on the southern flank occupy the wooded slopos at the head-waters of the 
Amliwa and Khutnu gfds, tributaries of tho’ Jumna, with the exception of the 
Kh4rsi and Punéha blocks, which form part of the Bawar range From Snow View 
B prominent spur runs westwards to Moila Tomple, 9,058 feet, its lowest point being 
at Lokandi Lani, 8,340 feet. The whole area is broken up by numerous secondary 
spurs (offshoots of the two main ridges) which again sub-divide, and it is furrowed 
by a great number of tributaries of the streams mentioned above, each of these boing 
in its turn fed by a number of smaller nalas, most of which bave running water in 
them only in the rains. 


There are large areas of precipitous and rocky ground, much of which is unwork- 
able, over 3,000 acres having been classed as unproductive on this account (vide 
Appendix D). They are found principally nt southerly, south-eaaterly, and south- 
westerly aspects in Inéri, Marauwa, Kutnidr, Jadi, Darna, Mats fugra, Mobna, Durani, 
Ekra, Kulered, Amlokri (compartment 3), Rikta and Legtna (mostly compartment 1), 
and there aro, besides, smaller unproductive areas in nearly all the other blocks. 
Darna 4 and Dulidua 4 were excluded from the valuation aurvey on account of their 
worthleasness, but the few trees dotted about on the other precipitous ground men- 
tioned above havo beon enumerated. At such aspects the slopes are as a rule rugged 
and precipitous, the limestone rocks often forming very conspicuous cliffé, as in the 
higher part of the Inari block, along the Durani dhér in Kutuiar, along the main 


( 15 }: 


ridge from Darna l&ni to Chakrdita View, at the boar garden khad to Matkdngra, 
along the HujAwa and Moti dhars, there being at the latter locality, in the Dulidna 
block, a tremendous clitf, several hundred feet high, which towers above the slopes 
below, and in the higher part of Rikta. In the Moila block, at a westerly aspect, 
there is a line of stupendous limestone cliffs along the main dhar, with two similar 
walls branching off from it, and there are smallor cliffs forming ledges (na the upper 
parts of the Bajamdra and Leguna blocks. The rest of the area ag more or less 
‘southerly aspecta, though workable, is nevertheless usually very steep and often rocky 
and rugged. 


Precipitous ground is also’ not wanting at more or less northerly aspecta: for 
example, in the higher portion of the Missau block aboye the Simla road, where 
limestone cliffs are very prominent, in most places along the matn ridge, and in parts 
of the Darna, Danda, and Bajamara blucks. The majority of the slopes at this aspect 
are, however, fairly moderate and frequently even: e.g., large areas in Missdu, Chitri, 
Hajawa, Duliéna, and Pandawa blocks, also Kopti 3 and 4, extending down the 
Marauwa block to a short distance below Chaina chak, parts of Kanjatra about Kabitra, 
&c., Jéidi 7, northern parts of Mobna, Durdéni, Daénda 1, part of Kulered ahove 
the Bingér g4d, and most of the northerly slopes in Legéna, Konain, Karéli (upper 
part), Siari, Gutugéd, Bhujkoti, and Karamba. On auch slopes most of tho dhars 
are flat-topped, sloping down gently to the streams, ¢. g., Auni and Matuli dhdrs io 
Missau, the spurs on the right flank of the Durani dhér, the Bari dhér in Kanjitra, 
the spurs on either side of the upper portion of the Tika dhf&r in Konain, and the 
minor spurs generally in Duliana 1 and 2, Kopti 3 and 4, in Pandawa and in the 
part of Karéli above the fire line, It is on such ground that we find shallow 
@ater-courses, whercas elsewhere the sides of nalas aro usually steep, especialy at 
southorly aspects, and are uot unfrequently precipitous, as aloog the Paniawa and 
Marauwa g&ds im Kanjadtra, along the Seli nau and the Biti gad in Matkdngra, and in 
many places along the Hajdwa géd and its feeders, 


The dhérs exposed to the warmer aspects are usually sharp-edged, steep and 
often rugged, as are also many on nertherly slopes. The top of the great western 
spur near Moifa temple for an average width of ten chaine is nearly flat or has very 
gentle slopes, whilst similar ground, but of less width, occurs along the main ridge 
near Snow View. 


The gradient usually varies from 30 to 45 degrees and over, but gentler glapea: 
from 15 to 830 degrees are occasionally met with, as over some ,of the northerly 
slopes referred to ubove. 


The configuration of the Bodiar block is peculiar, as it is surrounded on the 
north, north-west, and south-east by high ridges, and there are also other prominent 
ridges along or near its south-western boundary, so that the central portion lies in a 
hollow which one might imagine to have been the bed of an old lake; a similar 
hollow, but in miniature, occurs in the Kandsar block. 


Owing to the limestone formation, landships are rare and perennial springs and 
nalas are not very numerous, ¢.g.,in the Bodidr block there are only two perennial 
springs, both close to the bungalow, but tbe larger streams always have running water 
in their lower course. 


36. Cantonment and outlying oak foreats.—In these forests, which lie between 
5,200 and 7,300 feet, there is very little rugged or precipitous ground and only 27 
acres in Béndla can be classed as unproductive. The gradients vary from 20 to 45 
degrees and are, on the whole, less than in the upper forests.

38. The geology of Jaunsir, has been discussed by Mr..R D.. Oldham ina + 
note contained in the Reegpds of the Geological Survey. of India, Volume XVi,, Part * 
4, 188i from which the following gparticulare congested. with -  Deoban un range! 
have been extracted sam eget ie . os eo my 
6 B98. The Chakrdta series LT he oldest formation it Plansdr, provisionally called 
the Chakrata series, consists of groy slates and quartzites, underlai@ by a band of 
limestone generally some 300 or 400 feet thick, Which is again underlaid by a great 
series of slates and quartgites marked by the prevalence of red and mottled beds, : 
The principal exposures of the limestone lie in a zone running about east and \ y 
and pass immediately to the south of the station of Chakrita; to i Mnorth “BP 
zone the hills are formed of ‘the underlying red Chakrata slates and quartzites, 
while to the south the upper grey slutes are exposed, notwithstanding the prevailing” : 
; northerly dip of the beds. This is but part of the great Himalayan puzzle, that 
 pewembeds almost always seem to dip under older, that faults are generally reversed, 
and that the dip @f the beds in their neighborhood is precisely the reverse of what 
_ would be expected on a@ priori grounds. The total thickness of these beds is undeter- 
“minable, partly on account of their invense disturbance and partly from the fact 
that neither their base nor summit has been seen, but it must amount to many thou- 
sands of feet, 


on 

“In northern Jaunsér there is another expusure of the same beds intersocted ‘by 

a great fault which, first appearing from underneath the Deoban limestone near the 
village of Konain, runs north-westerly to Mudhaul on the west of the Tons, 


‘In. several parts of Jaunsér voleanic beds are exposed in tho Chakrata series 
which promise to be of great value in tracing out the geology of the lower 
‘Himilayas. Sy : ‘ | 

40, “ The Deoban series.—Overlying the Chakréta series comes a grest thickness 
of limestones and dolomites so similar to the Krol series as to be almuot certainly 
gontemporaneous with it, but which, as its rélation to the underlying beds is very 
different to what has been described on the Simla section, will be provisionally 
called the Deoban series, Lithologically it consists of a great thicknoss of bluish- 
grey bedded limestones, some of the beds, as on the aseent from Deoban to Chakrata, 
containing many nodales of chert ; others, which are generally ‘mep! tic, have a 
peculiar pisolitic stracture, being composed of small round black nodules cemented 


(17) 


by a white calcareous matrix. A,peouliar structure in some of the beds makes them 
resemble an accumulation of some elosely-chambhered shells imbedded in a matrix 
of calcareous mud, and so orgunic-looking that it is difficult to believe that they 
are not ubscured fossils. A very considerable proportion of the beda is in some 
of the sections dolomitic, varying from a slightly magnesian limestone to a pure 
pale-grey erystalline dolomite. Interstratified with these calcareous beds is a varying 
proportion of slaty beds, occasionally colored, but as a rule grey. 


6‘ This series is quite unconfurmable to the underlying Chakréta beds, as is 
proved by its unconformably overlapping or overstepping their eroded edges. This 
is very well scen near Konain, where the Deoban lies on the Chakrata limestone, 
while, as the boundary is tracéd to the west, it is seen to rest successively on a 
(locally) descending sories of slates. 


41. “ The Munddli series. —Above the Deoban limestone comes a series of bads 
mostly conglomeratic, fiat identified by me in the neighborhood of the Mundali 
forest bungalow, after which it is proposed top call them, at any rate provisionally 
They consist of conglomerates mostly with a slaty matrix, through which pebbles of 
quartzite, slate, or limestone are scattered, though some, and in Southern Jaunsfr 
the majority, of the beds are not conglomeratic at all ; others are colored slates, not 
unlike indurated Sirmdrs, and others again are calcareous. Of the latter some are 
fine-grained limestones ; others, though this I have only seon near Mandali, are 
limestone conglomerates cemented by a limestone matrix. The presence of these 
pebbles derived from the underlying Deoban limestone is sufficient to stamp the beds 
containing them as unconformable to it. In Bonthern Jaunsir, in addition to the 
Jaunsir conglomerate with slaty matrix, which is not found in every exposure, the 
characteristic rock is a quartz grit containing fragments of indurated red slate from 
the lower Chakrata beds. 


The facies of the Mundalis is essentially littoral or shallow water, as is testified 
by their coarseness of grain, while the conglomerates with a slaty matrix, so similar 
to those of the Blaini age, could not have been formed except through the agency of 
floating ice; but it is nota little remarkable that, notwithstanding their evidently 
shallow-water origin, there is hardly an exposure which does not exhibit one or 
more bede of pure limestone. This association of littoral beds with limestone is well 
geen on the cart road to the north of Kalsi, where a thick band of limestone ia 
bounded on both sides by conrse-grained quartz grits. 


e 
‘The Mundélis are evidently later than the Deobén, and carlier than the main 
disturbance of the Him4layan rocks ; so much so that in the limestone area of 
Northern Jauns4r, the small patches that have been left owe their preservation to 
having been caught up in the folds and fuultings of the limestone, and in this way 
preserved from denudation. They are probably of pre-Tertiary age. 


A2. “The Néhan and Bdwar series.—Above the Mundélis are two series of 
rocks, of which, as they occupy totally distinct areas, it is impossible to say which 
is the older. Of these one is the Ndhan, occupying only a very small area in 
the extreme south of Jaunsdr, and the other is the Bawar, ocoupying a large area in 
Bawar. 


43. “ The glacial epoch.—This epoch has left its traces in Jaunsér, though 
no traces of actual glaciers are known east of the Tons. The only deposits that 
can be referred tu this epoch are the high-level gravels to be seen in most of the 
valleys, but most distinctly in that of the Seli gad, where, as can be seen from the 
Chakréta and Mussoorie road, they for'm broad, gently-sloping terraces on the valley 
sides.” 


44. Hock and soil of the Deoban forests.—In the forests north of Chakrata the 
chief rock is the Devban limestone, which forms all the precipices and is often greatly

developed along dhars and the sides of naias. In almost every block it is mixed with

predominate, us m the lower parts of the Cldétri and Karfli blocks and along the 
lower course of the Marauwa gad. They are usually soft and crumbling and of a 
grey or bluish color, but some are hard and often reddish, as in Indri, Missau (com- 
partment 7), in parts of Asmari and Thona, along the Marauwa gad and in other 


places. 


In compartment 5, Hojdwa, there ia a bed of considerable thickness of blue and 
purple shules, the lower strata of which are slaty, and the bed during the rains is 
covered with a yellowish-white efflorescence of alum salts, 


Overlying the limestone along many broad flut-topped dhfrs with gentle slopes, 
on rounded peaks or caught up in the folds of the limestone, are Leds of clay-slates, 
soft und usually of a yellowish but sumotimes of a bluish color, which belong to 
the Mundah series. Such beds are to be found near Bojia l4ni and in numerous 
other places in the Bodiér block, on the flat ground near Moila temple, and along the 
upper part of the Gazat-ki-dhér in the same block, in parts of the Missau block, ulong 
the Missau road, on Kopti tiba, Odna tiba, and other adjacent peaks, along the Bari 
dbér, in many places along the Gobras-ki-dhar in Darna, peur Seli lani, in places in

tiba, where there are large holes from which ore used to be excavated, and in places in 


the Pand4éwa block. 


There aro outcrops of quartzites in a few places as in the Infri block along thd 
main ridge, along the Mashuk gid, along the Kashmoi gad in Durdni, where hard, 
crystalline, bluish quartz-lke rocks are exposed, in Legina along the Daura gad, and 
also cropping out on some dhars and in the lower parts of Karéli. White quartzose 
rocks are also very abundant in the Sidéri block, along the Bherimara dhér and some 
of its off-shoets, and im the lower parts of Gutug4éd and Bhujkoti, 


In compartment 3, Chétri, there are huge bloeks of trap, and outcrops of it are 
to bo scen on the Tons road between Devban and Pandawa. 


Gulena is found at Konain in nodules between beds of limestone, but it is very 


local in its distribution. 
e 


The subsoil is usually a free and randy clay somctimes loamy and frequently 
stony. It is often extremely shallow or altegether absent on precipitous ground or 
on sharp-edged rngoed dhars ; it is also vory shallow on some poorly-stocked apursa, 
brt on many dhérs it is fairly deep and sometimes crops out to the surface ; it is also 
usually deep in ravines, on most ffut-topped spurs, on rounded peaks, and on almost 
all gentle slopes, Leing in some places over thiee fect deep. In many localities, how- 
ever, the subsoil is a rather stiffish clay, such, for exumple, as the yellow clay which 
covers large areas in Bodiar and which is much developed in the low-lying portions 
and on gentlo slopes along nalas, us also near the Moila temple. Rather stiff clay is 
found in the open blanks in Missau, Marauwa 1 and 2, and in parts of Thona, where 
bands of it occasionally crop out to tho surface, in Kanjitra, on the peaks along the 
main ridge and along the dirirs and in open grassy blanks in compartments 1 and zy, 
Jadi, and on peaks in Mobna and generally on slopes to the north of the Rangni 
khad, in Durani 6, over a groat portion of the Ikra, Kolered and Hojawa blocks, 
in the eastern part of Panddwa 3, along the Tika dhér in Konain and in parts of 


Kardmba. 

The top-roi], where it existe, is mostly loam uaually free, dark and fainly deep 
(from bne and a half to two feet) under well-stocked more or less northerly slopes, 
cud along deep moist ravines, ¢. g., parts of Bodiar, Miesao, Thona, Jadi, Darna, Mohna, 


( 19 ) 


Hajawa, Pandéwa, Legina, Konain, Gutueid, Bhujkoti, Karamba and in many other 
places, as well as occasionally on some southerly slopes at high altitudes, as im the 
stocked portion of Kutnidr. Generally, however, it is poor and very shallow or often 
altogether absent at the warmor aspects, along many dhirs, in open grassy blanks, 
and on most of the precipitous ground. Tlicre is scarcely any loam in the chfr 
forests. 


Except along some of the nalas and on some damp northerly slopes, humus 
is usually absent from the areas open to fires, ns, for instance, in the Marauwa, Ekra, 
Kulered, Amlokri and Déuda blocks, and in the portions stocked with chir in the 
Chitri and Kuréli blocks. This is also the case on many exposed slopes, as at 
more or less southerly aspects m Thonn, Matkangra, Durani and Loguna, or at best 
there is only a very thin layer as on similar slopes in Konuin, 


On well-stocked northerly slopes protected from fire and under most of tho 
karshu forest there is often, on the contrary, a deep, rich lnyer of humus as ee 
Kutniar, Mohna, Hajawa, Daliana, Panddwa, Konain, Siéri, Gutugdd, Bhujkoti, 
Kurimba and some other blocks, but on others there is only a slight quantity, «. a 
in Moila, Asm4ri, Darna, and Karéli, In Bodifr the soil is frequently covered with 
a layer of half decomposed needles from three to six inches deep, very inimical to 
natural production, and this is also the case in some other localities, as, for instance 
on northerly and north-westerly slopes in Asmiri and in parts of Konaia. i 


45. Rook and soil in the Cantonment and detached oak forests.— Here the principal 
rocks are shales, slates and quartzites and occasionally outcrops of hard metamorphic 
rock, bolonging to the Chakrita series and in Bandla and Kurwa, partly also to the 


Mandalia. 


All these small oak forests are situated close to bands of the Chakrita limestone 
from which lime is prepared at Kurwa by the Military Works Department. 


The soil is generally a free, sandy loam mixed with large pieces of the under- 
lying rocks. It is shallow on ridges but deep in ravines, and in many open places 
and dhara it is clayey. There is a small quantity of humus in nalas and in the 


woll-stocked parts. 


46. Rock and soil in the Kdlsi forest.—At Kalsi there is a small development 
of the Nahan beds, this being the only locality in which they are found in Jaunsér. 
Above them there are some hard shales, quartzite grits and metamorphic rocks 
belonging to the Mundéli series, whilst on the chaor at the foot of the Panjgion 
block and on the flat ground at the junction of the Jamne and Tons there are 
some recent river deposits. 


The Naéhan beds consist mainly of a soft, coarse, micaceous sandstone, forming 
the Haripir ridge and tho slopes on either side of it, as also the detached hillock 
on the opposite side of the Amliwa. It is occasionally covered by beds of brown 
or red marl which disintegrates into small pieces on exposure to tho atmosphere. 
Detached angular pieces of the sandstone are strewn all over the ground and near 
the Tons it becomes more compact, forming a valuable quarry from which ornamental 
stunes ure oxtracted, chiefly by the Military Works for use at Chakrdta. 


In one or two places along the Amldwa and in compartments 10 and 11 of 
Haripur there are a few beds of a Wuish, crystalline limestone belonging to the 
Mandalis. Tho recent deposits consist of thick beds of boulders and sand mixod 
with silt. 

, Tho soil is a dry, loore, sandy loani, mised with fragments of rock, shallow 
on the hillsides, especially along the ridges, but deep on tho level ground. Bands 
of clay crop out here and there aleng the Haripur ridge and also on the flat ground 
near the ‘uns.

48, The etock map.—In Appendices B. and C. will be found detailed descriptions 
of the blocks and compartments, and a stock map has beon prepared with the help of 
the enumeration survey by Forest Ranger Keshavanand, who also described the 
compartments. This map does not, of course, show every small change, but it 
represents fairly well the composition of the stock in each compartment. For the 
upper foresta deodér, kuil, chir, firs, oaks, kukut, cypress and ayér and burdns 
are shown by washes of different colors, and where any of the kinds mentioned, 
although not predominant, yet constitute at least one-tenth of the total number of 
trees, their presence is indicated by lines of the proper color. Thus a forest of 
deodar mixed with kail and oak is shown by a green wash representing deodar, 
the predominating species, and by blue and burnt sienna lises across it to show 
that it is mixed with kai] and oak. 


Bare, precipitous, unproductive ground which is not considered suitable for 
sowing or planting purposes hus been shown by a wash of Indian ink, and other blank 
areas which can be converted into forest have been left uncolored. Where such 
blank areas contain scattered trees or are being covered with reproduction, this is 
shown by large dots for the mature trees and small ones for the young plants, and 
plantations are similarly represented, but are surrounded by a boundary line. 


In tho Kalsi forest sal, kukét, b4kli, gos4m and haldu are shown separately, and 
the dota over the wash indicate the occurrence of large trees of the three latter 
species where they constitute less than one-tenth of the whole stock.

It will bo observed that the principal types of forests are those of deod4r, kail, 

firs, oaks and miscellaneous species, viz, chir, cypress, aydér, burAns and kuk&t in the 
upper hills, and totally distinct type of sil and khukat forest at Kalsi. These types will 


now be discussed separately. 
(1)--Deodar. 

49 Deodar is found in numerous places between 6,000 and 9,000 feet, but its 
distribution in this zone is very irregulur. The three blocks of 1st class forest, vis., 
Bodifr, Kanasar and Konain, are essontially deoddr ones, as are also about half of the 
Missau block, the upper and eastorn portion of the Karéli block, nearly half of the 
stocked area in Rikta, and small portions of the Ch&tri, Kopti, Marauwa, Duréni, 
Legitina, Sidri, and Gutugéd blocks. It also frequently forms more than one-tenth of 
the stock in the kail, fir and oak forests, both over parts of the above-mentioned blocks 
and also in parts of Moila, Asm4ri, Kunjatra, Bajamfra, and Bhujkoti, and is the 
only valuable part of the etock in the Kardmba and Panikand! blocks, Omitting 
these two last, for which no stock map has been prepared, the area of deodfr-producing

It will be seen that the numbor of good timber trees at preaont available is very 

small compared to the total number of trees over six fevt in girth. This is doubtless 

due to the oontinnous felling of the finest trees and to the forests having been exposed 

to fir and giazing in the past. Around many of these branchy trees thre is a fine 


( 23 ) 


crop of young dendir, and it is of sylvicultural importance to remove the old ones 
as 800n as posrible. 


The number of the younger elasses is very satisfactory and with continued 
protection a large number of good well-growa timber trees with long clean boles will 
be produced eventually.

Speaking of the two firs, Sir D. Brandis in his Preliminary Working Plan 
says :— 

“ (1) The Himdlayan Spruce, Rai (Abies Smithiana).—The hanging cones ripen 
and the secd falls in October or November, It is believed that in the Droban forests 
a full crop of seed generally occurs onoe in three or four years. Seedlings of the 
Himflayan spruce spring up readily and grow up vigorously in open pla es without 
shelter, provided that they are not too dry. Young plants up to 6 feet high ure 
often found under the shade of largo trees, but they do not generally look vigorous, 
and make no progress until sufficient ght is given. On tho other hand, where 
sheep and goats allow it to come up, young growth of all ages is found on the edge 
of the forests and on perfectly bare slopes. Under these circumstances, it will be 
necessary in forests mainly consisting of spruce to give much light when cuttings 
aro made, with the view of ensuring the natural reproduction of this tree. This 
remark mainly relates to slopes with a more or lese northerly aspect, at the mean 
elevation of the Thona and Kanjatra blocks. It is not impossible that in other 
foresta on different rock, at a lower elevation and in a different climate, this tree 
may require a different treatment, 


% Young spruce is readily eaten by sheep and goats, and in many places we 
found this to be the cause of the stunted appearance of the young trees. Old stems, 
like those of tho European spruce, are, under certain circumstunces not yet oxpluined, 
aubjoct to dry rot in the contre. It is probable that plantations of this tree will 
aucoved easily, aud that it can with advautage be employed to stock open grassy slopes 
particularly with a northerly aspect. 


“ (2) The Himdlayan Silver Fir, Morinda ( Abies Pindrow).—The erect cones 
broak to pieces, aud suales and seeds full in Uctober or the beginning of November. 
Like the European silver fir, young troes of the morinda require shelter and live 
for a long time under deep shade, maintaining a bright green colur and a healthy 
appearance, but making little progreas until light is yivon overhead, when they 
produce vigorous terminal shoots, Wherever natural reproduction on & regular 
system is atlempted iu these forests, success will probably be certain in the case of the 

iD 


( 26 ) 


silver fir, and on that account morindu trees likely to shed sead should be spared in 
all cuttings as much as possible. Old stems are generally sound, and there is no 
ground to anticipate thut the wood will be less acceptnble as fuel than that of the 
spruce.” 


(4)—The Oaks. 


52, Four species of onk occur: karshu, moru, ban and inal, of which the first 
three only are important, and they occupy two very marked zonos: (a) the karshu 
forosts, which occur above the fir zone in a narrow strip along the main ridge and its 
jurge westerly spur, extending for a considerable distance down the slopes at easterly 
and more or leas southerly aspacts, as in the higher parts of the Kutniér, Jidi, 
Mohna, Hajawa and Pandéwa blocks, whilst smuller areas are also met with in com- 
partment 1, Karamba, and on southerly slopes for a short diatnnce below Kopti tiba 
in the Kopti blook ; (5) the lower onk forests, consisting almost sololy of ban mixed 
abundantly with ayér and burans, which often predominate numerically and which 
occur in the Cantonment forests and in the outlying oak forests, as well as in the 
lowest part of Missou and between the chfr and deodar zones in Chaétri mixed with 
chir, whilst there is a mixed onk forest of ban and moru over nearly all tho stocked 
areas in lnéri, Darna, Matkanygra, Ekra, Kulered, Amlokri and Dandy, as woll as 
in the lowest parts of Moiln, Thona, Maranwa, Jédi, Mohna, Duréni, Legana, Karéli, 
Sidri, Gutuzad, Rikta, Pandéwa, Duliana, and Hajéwa. At the lowest slevation’ bén 
is alnost the sole oak, moru mixing in higher up and being common along gfds, 
but nowhere forining lurge masses of furest. 


There are very few inai, and these are found mainly along nélas at the lowest 
clevations, as, for example, in Béndla and Amlokri. 


Roughly spenking, the oaks may be snid to occur in this range between the

Spenking of the korshu forests in 1878, Sir D Brandis said —“ The seed mpens 
early 10 the rains, but at this elevation (Muhna, 9,000 fect) the karsha does not seed 
every year There was no seed in 1874, but there was a heavy crop in 1473, and 
consequently the ground under the treea 18 now everywhere covered with seedlings of 
that year, most of which will of course perish unless hght1s given Noarly every block 
in the foreat 1s covered with young growth of more advanced age, and 1t seems probu- 
ble that karshu forests can readily be reproduced by means of two successive cuttings, 
provided a good seed year precedes or follows the first cutting The extent of the 
first cuttung must be determined by experience , sufficient hght must be given to 
ennble the seedlings to giow up, and sufficient trees must be left for seed and partial 
Bhelter As itis desirable tor many reasons to encourage the growth of mixed forest, 
all vigorous coniferous trees scattered im karshu forests should be spurcd at tho time 
ot the frat cutting ” 


The number of unsound onk 18 very large, especially in the case of morn, where 
nearly halt of the lst class, one third of the 2ud class and one-fourth of the Srd cluss 
trees come under this categury.

Tha Cantonment Blocks. 


59. From the Guzotteer we learn that previous to 1866 the site of the present 
Cantonments consisted of a range of grass-clad hills with more or less dense onk 
forests running up on all sidea from the valleys below. The ground was used solely 
for grazing and a few loosa sheds here and there the only signs of habitation. The 
supposed unlimited supply of good water at a high level on the Deoban range of 
hilla warked the place as in every way suitable for a military station, nnd operations 
were commenced in 1866, but no troops came until 1869, when some sappers were 
stationed here and were succeeded by the 55th Regiment. Since this date these 
forgats have become of importunee, as oak makes the heat fuel and the forests are 
olose at hand, wiilst the nrea of oak forests closed to fires and grazing under the 
control of the Korest Depurtinent js limited compared with the other clusses of forest. 


The blocks which it is proposed to lease from the Cantonment Committee (wide 
paragraph !61) are stocked with ban ouk and anunderstage of burdns and ayar, with 
some kukét along the nalas, whilet a few pines are beginning to appear since fires 
have been excladed ‘Lhe stock is nawhere dense, unless where it fringes some of 
the largor nalus, and it is often excessively open along dhars and nt exposed aspects 
generally, whilst there are numerous grassy blanks qccupying the flanks of dhaérs 
with easterly, sonth-easterly and southerly aspects, such blinks being either nltoge- 
ther devuid of tree growth or dotted with only a few ban, ayér, and burans here and 
there. 

In the two blocks Lurli and Sntoli, which have been closed to grazing for the 
past eight years, reproduction, although somewhat patchy, is very good for all species, 
and the young plants are doing well, but there are very few established seedlings in 
the grassy blanks, 


Jn the three blocks open to grazing, which it is proposed to take up, reproduoc- 
tion is also good as far as numbers ure concerned, but the young ban are excussively 
browsed by cattle except ou steep places and are in some spots reduced to mere 
bushea, Aydr and burdns seedlings are very common, but they are scarcely touched 
by grazing animals, the young leaves of aydr being very poisonous. It is to ba 
feared, in consequence, that, if indiscriminate grazing be allowed, the valuable oak. 
forest will be replaced by the other two species, both of which are of sgl any 
value even for fuel.

Influence of altitude. 


60. This influence is vary marked in the Doobfin range, extending as it does 
from 1,590 feet at Kalai to 10,075 feet nt Karamba tiba. At Kala: we find the sal 
and its associates, the highest point to which they exten! being tn the Birabagh bluck 
up to an elevation of about 2,500 feet. On uscending the OhikrAti oart road this 
vegetation soon gives place to a somewhat scrubby growth of Bo-hmeria, buuhiniae, 
Woodfordia, &. Higher up, between 4,500 and 7,000 feet, thore is the zono of bin 
ook, rhododendron and aydr, between 7,000 and 5,50) feet the mora vak, and trom 
8,000 feet tothe highest puint of the range the knrshu oak Arnougst the conifers 
ebir is found within forest limits from 5,000 to 6,00 ) feat, deodar and kail from 6,009 
to 8,000 feet, rai and morinda from 8,000 feet and upwards, The kul zone 1s, however, 
shghtly lower than that of the deodar, the rat than that of the morinda, although 
for the moat part they are found mixed together, The limits here given ure fre- 
quently exceeded by individual members of the diffurent species, and the influsnoe of 
altitude 14 often considerably modified by the aspoot, soil, drainage, dv. Lhus on the 
northern slope of a rulge the moru oak may come up to the sum uit, whereas on the 
warmer slopes of the same rdge there may bo nothing but ban, und this is well seun 
along the spur on which the Mindaéli bangulow 1s built. Again on the hot dry slopes 
of the Bodidr torest deodér extends up to 9,000 feet. 


The herbaceous vegetation changes with the altitule in an equally markel 
manner, and in the highest zone nearly all the forms are closely allied to European 
ones; it ts here that primulas, anemones, ranuncali, balsims, &c., abound, together with 
quantities of maiden-luir and other ferns. A genus not found in Kurope, Strobilan‘hes, 
grows in dense musses under shady forest, and there are two gregarious bimbuos, viz., 
the emall ringdél (Arundinarta faleata) between 5,000 and 7,00U feet, and the large 
ring&l (Aru adinaria spathylora) trom 7,500 to 10,000 feat. 


Lufluence of aaspret and slope. 


61. The effect of aspect in these hills is well seen when looking towards Deoban 
from Chakruta. In front are bare, gras y and rocky slopes, whilst just appearing 
above the main ridge 1s a long row of firs, the top of the comferous torests whick 
clothe the slopes facing north. The foresie wiich do exist on the southerly slopes 
above Chakidta ccnsiat of the two oaks, mora and bin, and their associates, 
rhododendron ayar, &c, whilst u aa ouly behind the inain ridge that wo find the firs 
in abundance. 


The greater portion of tho extensive grassy blanks found in the range face morse 
ar less south, aad wherever there are turests fueciug the south, suuth-west or soutie 
east, thay are usually very open, the chief exception bemg in the case of the karshu, 
which forms nlmost pure aud generally dense forests at these aapects in the highwat 
parts, v.¢., Kutoiar, Hujawa 6 and 8 Qtherwise the denvest forests are wet with 
at northerly, north-westerly, and north-ourterly aspects. 


At tho same altitude throughout the forests to the north of the main ridge from 
Duran to Bhujkoti, 1¢1s common to find doodar predominating on the more or less 
southerly slope, which are invariably dteep and rocky or even preeipitous, the firs on 
«he more or less northerly ones. Cypress only grows on the most precipitous and 
rocky pluces, chiefly in the Mois block, and deodir likes steep zradients, preferring 
slopes of over 25°, unless there 18 exceptionally good drainage and tho foredt 1 a dry 
one us in parts of Bodiar. 


Influence of locality and soile 


62. The most striking effeat of leoality is to be sean in the narrow fringe of 
broad-loaved trees which border the nalad, the slopes on either side being clothed 
ewitth conifers. The cause of this is doubtless the deeper and better soil uoar thse 


streams as well as the greater moisture. 
9D 


( 34 ) 


The finest karshu forests are situated on the rounled tops of the highest hills, 
whore hyht is abundant and ¢he soil deep nod somewhit oluyey. The cypress is 
supposed to have a preference for a oalonreous, the two pines for a siliceous soil, 
whilst the deodér is indifferent, provided the soil is not too stiff and the drainage 
gool. The onks and the spruce thrive well, on the contrary, on clayey ground, the 
sotl having to be deep for the former to attain large dimensions. Box is only found 
in the damp shady valleys between Jadi und Chakratae. The presence or nbsence of 
forest also exercisvs a most marked influence ; thus the yew is only to be found under 
the dense cover of taller trees, morinda does not come up in the open, whorcas this 
is a necessity for young pinos. 


Quality and sise of the trees. 


63. Most of the mature deodér are very branchy, knotty and unfit for timber, 
as they have grown up in open forests exposed to fires and to unrestricted grazing $ 
but the yoanger classes are more promising and a large number of valuable young 
troes are growing up for future consumption, especially in those places where the 
natural seedlings have come up so thickly that they now form dense young pole 
forests, the polos already having lost a number of their lower branches Thus out 
of some 40,000 Ist-class trees in this range it is ostimated that not quite one-tenth 
are good timber trees. The best trees now available are found in the Karamba, 
Gutugdéd and Bhujkoti blocks, having grown up umidst a dense forest of firs, &c. 


For similar reasons most of the large kail are not of good quality, but there 
are a very large number of well-grown firs, especially rui, capnble of furnishina excele 
lent kartis, planks, &., and it is regretablo that the present demand is limited to the 
small requirements of Chakréta. The best blocks for good spruce are Karimba, 
Bhujkoti, Gutugad, Kopti, and Bodifr. 


As the ouks are only used for fuel their quality is not of much importance, and 
the vast majority are crooked and badly grown, whilst nearly half the Ist-clase 
trees and a very large proportion of the 2nd and Srd class ones are unsound. 
In the case of very lurge trees, however, knottiness ta a disadvantage, as it prevents 
them being split easily, and many of thom have to be made into charcoal as the 
easiest meuns of utilizing them. <A great number of oaks have been dam aged by 
lopping. Generally speaking, in the closed forests there is a marked imprevement 
im the quality of the young growth compared with that of the older trees, 


Forest Ranger Keshavanand measured the heights of a number of trees of 
various kinds in different compartments, and his tneasurements are given in Appendix 
KR. The deod&r goes up to 150 feet in height ynd 15 feet in girth, whilst tho two firs 
attain in places u height of nearly 290 feet and # girth of 15 feet. A rai which the 
forest students measured in Kopti was found to be 170 feet bigh und 14 feet in girth, 
and it was estimated that 1t contained 1,500 stucked cubic feet of wood. In Inari a 
moru was measured 142 feet high and 12 8 feet in gitth, whilst a bin in the saine 
block was found to be 113 feet high and 16 feet girth ; but these two oaks usually 
run to from 50 to 90 feet high, with w girth of 6 to 9 feat. In the forests below 
Chakr&ta, however, the ban seldom exceeds 80 feet in height and 45 feet in girth. 
The karshu is, as a rule, a smaller tree, the biggest measured being two trees in Leguna, 
one 91 feet high and 101 feet in girth, the second 71 feet in height and 116 feet 
in girth, but the usual size of mature trees is from 50 to 80 feet high and 5 to 7 feet 
round. 

Buréns sometimes acquires a large girth, but seldom grows more than 35 feet in 
height. One tree in Darna had a girth of 9 feet and a height of 49 feet,

The number of 5th class kukft is relatively high in each case, but a large 
number of them are mere bushes und can never attain to Ist or 2nd class dimensions., 


It will be observed that the forests open to fires ani grazing are sadly deteriorat- 
ing, since the number of 5th class trees ought almost always greatly to exceed the 
number of 1st and 2nd class ones in irregular forests, anch as those we are dealing with 
here. Tho only excoption is in the number of young ayar and burans, and as these 
almost worthless species are always nssociated with bin oak, it would seom as if under 
the séyime of fires and grazing they are gradually usurping the place of the more 
valuable oak. 


In the forests closed to fires only reproduction, though vastly superior to that in 
tho non-fire-proteoted parts, is yet not ao good as in those closed to grazing as well, 
with the solitary exception of the fire. The reason for this exception lies doubtless in 
the fuct that all the closed forests, besides being often very dense, so that soedlings 
are not required, are at hiyh altitudes and at cold aspects, where reproduction beeomes 
difficult and seed years are rare, whereas the open fir forests are lower down, many of 
them being below 8,500 foet, 


In the open blocks the irregular manner in which the forests are reproduced is 
grently enhanced by the erratio way in which they are grazed. The cowe and 
bullooks graze all the year round within a few miles of the villages, as do also nearly 
all the goata, for the latter can stand being housed and thus furnish valuable manure 
for the various grain orops. The sheep, on the contrary, are constantly on the move, 
all those of one khat usually grazing together. In the winter they go down to the 
bottom of the valleys below the suow and in the suinmer they are brought up to the 
high-level pasture lands hetweon 8,000 and 10,000 feet, where they remain from June 
to October, going once to the villages to be shorn. Throughout the forests there are 
regular halting-places for these flocks, called “ tuahes’’ ; these are open grassy blanks 
with gentle slopes surrounded by small pillars of loose stones, on whioh the shepherds 
light fires at night to ward off wild animals, There are, then, numerous congested 
grazing centres scattered throughout the open forests, and other heavily-grazed areas 
in the immediate vicinity of the different villages, whilst there are also considerable 
arcas where grazing is very slight or almost nil, the latter being the case on very 
precipitous ground or on leas steep places to which access is difficult. It has been 
observed along roads leading to water or to other grazing-grounds that the slopeg 
above ure often full of seedlings, whilst those below contain scarcely any, the 
explanation being that the flucks in going to and fro do not olimb up the steep 
out hanks, but almost invariably graze along the slopes below the oaths. 


In the kail zone reproduction is so vigorous that it is onty close to “‘taches” or 
other beavily-grazed places near the upper limit of the species wo need fear for the future 
of the forest. In such places there are numerous grassy blanks so closely cropped that 
the grass forms a dense turf, reminding one of English downs. Here, and it is the same 
for rai, the scattered kail and deodér seedlings are wach browsed down and keptin the 


( 37 ) 


form of bushes with branchos spreading out in all directions until the centre gets 
beyond the reach of a goat, when a strong leading shoot is sent up. Over the more 
lightly-grazed areus reproduction, althongh greatly checked, as ia proved by the figures 
in the preceding table, is so powerful and vigorous that we need not fear for the 
future of the forest, although its potential yield is certainly much lessened, 


As to chir, there ure no areas protected from grazing in the range, but the 
benefits of fire protection ure even more marked then in the case of any other species, 
excepting porhaps kail, and the small portion of the Raura block atocked with ohir, 
which has been protected from fire since 1872, contrasts most favorably with the 
surrounding burnt forests. Now that the number of cattle, sheep and goats to be 
admitted to our forests is about to be fixed, it will probably never be necessary to 
close the chir areas to grazing, provided that fires can be excluded with the aid of the 
graziers themselves. Of course there will be sinall, heavily-grazed areas which may 
have tv be closed for a time, but it is hoped that, thanks to the wonderful reproductive 
powers of this pine, closing to fires alone will assure the maintenance of the forests. 


However destructive indiscriminate grazing may be ina coniferous forest, it is 
still more dangerous amongst oaks, for goats like nothing better than succulent young 
oak leaves. Nearly all the b&n oak foresta are vlose to villages, and thus grazing is 
continuous in them throughout the year; the result is that reproduction is most 
backward and the young seedlings are contorted, badly grown and trequently reduced 
to mere bushes, thanks to the persistent nibbling of the goats. 


The karshu forests, being the most remote of all forests from villages and being 
only grazed over occasionally in the rains, are in a much better state, and seedlings 
of all ages are very numerous at all more or less southerly aspects. Young moru sre 
uluo frequently to be seen forming denso thickets on the lower side of the parent 


trees. 

At Kalsi reproduction is usually very backward for nearly all species oxcept 
sal, recent seedlings especially being rare and often totally absent, whilst there are 
estublisbed seedlings of gostm, kukét and others.in many places, but very few of 
bakli and haldu. There is, on the contrary, splendid reproduction of sl where this 
species occurs, the grouud being often literally hidden by 1nnumerable yearlings, but 
it is to be feared that most of them will be troddeo down by cattle. 


Causes of injury. 


66. Man and animale.—The chief causes of injury by.man, such as fires, grazing 
domestic animals, making clearances for cultivation and lopping trees for fudder or 
tor bedding, are discussed in detail elsewhere. Other customs have been alt gether 
stupped, such as tapping for resin, tuking large strips of bark off the trunks of fir 
trees for roofing temporary huts in the forest, and hacking green deodar and other 


conifers for ijuel or torches, 


There are very few wild animals in these forests and the damage dono by them 
is not very great. Bears peel off the bark of young kail and deodér ut certuin sousons 
of the year and break down big ouk branches in their search after acorns. Flying 
squirrels feed almost exclusively on acorns and young oak leaves. Lunogurs feed 
partly on acorns and on young fir cones, especially those of murinda. Rats and mice 
often eat a great many of the seeds sown in pits, and many blanks were mado 
amonyst the chir and kal sowings at Lurli trom this cause. 


The ill-success of the kail sowings at Bodiar ws due in a great measure to phea- 
sants, which aro very fond of pine seeds and also dig up many of tho recently germi- 
meted seedlings. The Himalayan nut-cracker is very destructive to kail and other 
cones ewhilat they are still on the trees, but these birds are not so common here as 
they are farther in theintenor, Groxbeaks are similarly destractive. 


Not much ie yet known about the inspects in these forcats, but some of them by 
their numbers do a considerable amount of damage, The truit of Mistucta wmteyerrima 
10D 


( 38 ) 


is so much attacked by weevils that it is very difficult to get good seed In the same 
wavy last year a portion of the pure karshu forest in Mola 4 was sown up with 
nourns, the result being an almost complete failure owing to the fact that nearly every 
acorn contained one or more esterpillars belonging to x species of carpocapsa and 
closely allied to tho codlin moth, whose Jarvw are so injurious to apples. These 
acorns nre also attacked by gall-flies, and in future sowings it will be advianble to 
collect the acorns from some other locality and to examino them carefully, the 
diseased ones genorally having a small perforation like a minute pin- hole somewhere 
on the surface. The young and tender top-shoots of the morindn are frequently 
killed by some species of aphidw which surround the young stems and suck out all 
their juices, but ladybirds, which are numerous, help to keep them down. The bude 
of the rai are pierced by a species of cynips, the resulting gall being the facsimile 
of a fir cone in miniature. Oak galls are also pretty common, and there are others 
on indigofera, roses, &c. The grub of a beetle, probably a cockchaler, is a great post 
ju deoddr nurseries, destroying the roots of the young seedlings. 


67. Fungi.—Dr. Mayr, during his visit to Jaunsar in 1886, made the following 
notes: “In the Lurli plantation there a1e two kinds of fungi dangerous to the 
young Pinus longifolia. The first, Hysterivm pinanive, killa the young plants in 
winter and spring when not quite one year old, the needles becoming perfectly red. 
The needles are first green with yellow spots which spread out and color the whole 
needle red. If all the leaves are attacked the plant is killed. Asa rule about 5 per 
cent of the plants iu a nursery escape because the uppermost leaves are not attacked. 
The attacked leaves fall to the ground; and in wet soil the fungus begins to ripen, 
the yellow spots becoming perfectly black, and the epidermis opens in a Jongitudinul 
line, the spores of the fungus being shot out, so that at any wet season infection 
can tuke place, though generally only when the young leaves are produced. The 
infection generally attacks the young plants next season, leaving about 5 per cent., and 
these when threo years old escape entirely if isolated, but not if in clumps, as the spores 
cannot reach them. This fungus is entirely purasitic on the needles of one, two 
and three-year-old plants and only saprophytic on the fallen needles of all pines. 
It only attacks two and three needle pines, especially the latter. No proventive 
measures can be adopted except to isolate the plants as soon as possible, to plant out 
in the firat year and to make small nurserios. ; 


‘The other fungus ia Peridermium Pint, but alternates with Coleospermum Sene- 
cionis. The lutter appears in the wiater on the dead tenves of u senecio lying on the 
ground, forming a yellowish spot. The winter spores aro close together in a mass. 
These spores begin to germinate in apring, form a little tube with a spore at its 
extremity, which is blown to the new leaves of a pine of any age, up to 15 or 20 feet 
in height. It germinates on a pino, entering the stomata, and grows on the needle, 
forming a stroma, like the preceding fungus, on the surfuce of the needle. The fungus 
mycelium furms a bag protruding through the epidermis of the needle and filled with 
orange spores which ure blown by the wind on to the leaves of a senccio. If the 
fungus attacks one-year-old seedlings, needles and stem they are killed. Two-year- 
old plauts regularly escape and older plants are attacked, but do not suffer. The 
mycelia can live in the needles for two or three years and produce stromas, but for 
a new infection sénecio spores are necossary : hence destruction of senecio 1s all that 
is necessary. The fungus sometimes attacks the bark of older trees through a wound 
by an insect, &., and then it 1s perfectly perennial, living in the bark and sapwuod. 
The fructification is exactly the same as on the leaves. Bianches and young plants 
are killed after three or four years, terminul shoots after five years. ‘Tho upper parts 
of large trees are killed after a jong fight, the wood trying to beat the fungus and 


forming a flat-shaped tree.” r 


Peridermium aoicolum is a fauugus abundant on rai and is described by Mr. Baden- 
Powell as a growth like a series of small fat sacs, or flattened teeth, growing ont of 
the needles, aud of a salmon color ; the outer sac is waved aud regular and the size us 


( 39 ) 


to brendth variable. The needles are also covered with brown spots, The fungus 
grows till the leaf turns quite yellow, and you can distinguish the trees at a distance 
by these yellow tassels. In this state the envelop b irsts, distributing an orange- 
colored powder aud leaving ragged fragments of the pellicle attached to tho dead or 


dying needles, 


The branches of FRreris oralifolia (ay4r) aro frequently covered with a brilliant 
magenta fungus like mags. This is a curious condition of matamorphosed tissue, in 
which cella are extended in the form of thick-clubbed hairs, in this instance of a 
beautiful magenta eolor The oondition thas represented is very cominon in temper- 
ate countries on the leaves of deyiduous trees, and formerly was regarded as u fungus 
of which there ware many forms described under the name of Erinewia. This peculiar 
diseaso has never been satisfactorily explained, except to show that it is an extension 
of the tissues of the plant itself and not a parasite. 


The disease which bursts throngh the cuticle of the loaves of Rhododendron 
arboreum and along the mid-rib much more resembles a fungus. The gregarivus 
ovnvex bodies are of a waxy consistence and appear to spring from the ligneous 
portion of the petiole. They aro entirely composed of compact separate threads, closely 
adherent in a mass, There is no mycielum and no spores, no evidence of a fungus. 
As far as we can judge this is entirely a condition of atrophy, What is the predis- 
posing cause is not appirent. It may be that the stimulation has been caused by 
the puncture of an insect. 


68. Climbers, oreepers, §o.—Those in the upper forests, unlike the maljan and 
others in the #41 areas, do little or no damage. The commonest of the climbers and 
creepers are, ivy; vines, purticularly Vitis himdlayana; Schigandra grandiflora and 
Frous scandens. These perhaps do more gowd than harm by keeping the trunk 
moist and killing off the lower branches. 


The chief parasites are the mistletoe, Viacum album, which is very common on 
apricot trees, but is never found in the forests, Vesosm japonicum and Loranthus 
vestitus, both found on the ban oak. 


The dense horbaccous growth consisting of balsams, Labiates, Dipsacus, Polygonum 
and others offer a considerable check to natural reproduction, as do also brambles, 
roses and other shrubs xs well as the two ringéls. Perhaps, however, the most 
inimical plants in this way are the Strobilunthes, of which the two commonest 
species in this range are S, Wallioht: and S. alutus. Brandis suys that the former 
is a remarkably gregarious plant, covering extensive areas, to the exclusion of other 
plunts, under the deep shade of the karshu forest of Suow View. Jn Hajawa and 
elaewhere it is found under spruce and silvor fir. It is an undershrub, the stems 
subdivided into numberless branches which are jointed and break off readily at the 
nodes. In apring the tufts of this plant are two to three feet high, and present a 
mass of short stiff twigs, balf woody, with small, cordate, nearly glabrous leaves. At 
the commencement of the rains these tufts shoot furth the long herbaceous branches 
bearing flax panicles of blue flowers. The other species is found on more suuny 
slopes, and is common in the warmer part of the Mohna block, while it is almost 
unknown on the cooler slopes of Hajiwa. The plant is perennial, but the stems 
and brauches aro herbacevus ; the leaves are larger, long-acuminate and very hairy 


with glandular hairs. 


Mr, Smythies in his school notes for this season snys: ‘‘ Every year the upper 
herbaceous portion of §. Wal/tohti breaks off at the node, leaving the woody stem 
beneath, which increases in length by one or two inches annually until at the time 
of fluwering, when the plant dies. The woody portion is two to three feet high, the 
height of the whole plant being about five feet. The last seeding took place in 
1651-82; the new shoots and tho woody portion are now from six to eight inches 
high and about six years old. When the next flowering will take place we do not 


( 40 ) 


know, but it is believed that the whole term of this plant is abont 12 yenra. Jn all 
probability it is only whon this plant dies down that the young karshu seedlings are 
able to force their way successfully through the dense network formed by their routs. 
Tt is a favourite fodder for sheep. Whon this speices flowered in 1881-82 a piece 
of ground at Snow View was entirely cleared of it before the capsules ripened ; this 
spot remains clear and is now covered with karshu seedlings.” 


69. Storma and enow —A few trees are yearly uprooted by the winds, especially 
in the winter months, bat the dumege done is never very great, Trees are not uncom. 
monly struck by lightning, especially tall conifers, the course of the fluid being usual- 
ly marked by a narrow strip cut out of the bark as.if with a knife, although some- 
times the trees nre shattered to pieces. Fires have originated from this cause ond 
one is said to have occurred this year near Lambatrach. 


Hailstorms are rather violent in the spring and often destroy unprotected seedlings 
and break the tonder top-shoots of aome of the conifers. They are so destructive to 
the fruit orup that it is found necessury to cover the tops of all the trees in the forest 
garden with mutting. 


Snow-breaks are common in the higher forests, and long narrow gullies denuded 
of trees are formed down many of the forests, notably at Hajawa and Bujamfra, by the 
glacier-like sotion of the snow, which is also the cause of the curved stems so notice- 
able in most of the young trees. 


Thanks to the geological formation, landslips are very rare and the mountain tor 
renta usually keep to their beds even after the heaviest rainfall. 


Minor Produoe. 


70. Ringdls, ge—-Of the two ringdls, the larger, Arundinaria spathiflora, is 
alone of much commercial importance, and it is Jurgely exported from this range to 
the plains for hookab-stems, whilst a few are used for trout fishing-rods and some 
other purposes. The large ringal flowered in 1881 aud very numerous seedlings havea 
sprung up since, but no culms have vet been sent up from the new rhizomes. It 
flowered previously in 1848 and in 18638, so that its poriod of life is from 15 to 1s 
years. Tho small ring&l, Arundinaria falcata, flowered in 188) with similar resalta. 


Immediately after the flowering the export of the large ring&ls mounted from 
5,154 to 19,769 score, endeavors being made to extract us many as possible of the 
dead stems, and this extraction has been continued ever since until tho supply 
within ten miles of Chakrféta has been almost exhausted. The right to cut those 
ringdls is now let yearly to a contractor and should in futyre be sold by auction. 


There is but very little other minor produce, and this chiefly from Kalsi, including 
bakli and dhéura leaves, limestone and quarrystone, whilst from the upper forests 
come bundles of the muiden-hair fern, haneraj, ep., Adsantum; seeds of Vernonia 
anthelmintica, kalijiri ; dried rhizomes of Acorus calamua, bach ; root of Aconitum 
heterophylium, atis; root of Hedyohium spicatum, khapur-kachri ; oxcrescences on leaves 
and petioles of Pistasia inteyerrima, kukra-singi, and roots of Thalictrum folioloeum 
or Cessampelos pareira, pelijiri. Truffles are not uncommon in the autumn, cspecially 
in fire-protected areas under thickets of young deodur. The boys who tend the 
sheep and goats find them by going on all fours and following up their scent sike 
a dog.

of whom there are two, both of them from Ladak and commonly called Aughani. 
By them it 1s eartied on the backs of mules and ponies to the two fuel godowne ut 
ChakrAta, in which it 13 stacked by another contractor, and rem uns under the care of 
the forest subordinate in char,ze of the depdt until handed over to the Co:minissariat 
Departinent or sold to reudents, Transport is the great difficulty, all tho fuel for 
Chakréta having to be brought on the backs of mules or ponies and all timbor on the 
backs of men for a distance of frum 3} to 10 miles for fuel and 18 for timber.
