Fire protection has therefore been a great success, the only fires which have 
oceurred having usually beon small ones, due to such causes as carelossness on the 
part of the subordinate ostablishmont in burning the fire-lines, to n high wind, in one 
or two instances to shepherds and charcoal-burners, and in 1883-84 n fire was 
caused by some villagers whilst burning a dead body, but no case of incendiarism bas 
ever occurred. Therc are two dangerous seasons, one from soon after the end of 
the rains until the first fall of snow, and the other from March to the setting in of the 
rains. An early fall of snow and frequent showers in April and May are, however, 
great helps in warding off fire, and except in the lowest parts and on hot grassy 
slopes there is usually very little danger. Lines from 10 to 20 feet broad are burnt 
all around the outside of the forests, and there are alsu interior lines at Dodiér and 
Kanfsar. 

The annual cost varies between 8 to 4} pies per acre successfully protected. 


For an account of protection from grazing, lopping, &c., ve chapter If. on 
Forest Privileges. 
Areas sown or planted, 


80. An aren of nbout 678 acres has been sown or planted up in the range in 
numerous different blocks as detailed in Appendix D., and in uddition 52 acres have 
heen sown up in lines under forest in Kanjétra, Hajawa and Mohana, whilst 69 acres 
have been planted up in the Cantonment blocks (vide para, 59). 


Most of these plantations have been visited in three successive years, 1885-1887, 
by students from the Forest School, countings and measurements being made to 
determine their relative success, and the figures thus obtained will be given below. 


81. (1) Kurwa.—In 1878-74 some Australian acacias were planted and in the 
following year it was stated that 129 only were alive, but they were flourishing ; none, 
however, now remain. Sowing of inai (Q. annulata), a few trees of which aro to be 
found amongst the natural stock, were mude in 1874 and 1875 in both compartments, 
somo b&n being alao dibbled in one of the plots. In 1883 some of the old pits wore 
sown with acacias (A. melanorylon, implexa and decurrens), and although some germi- 
nated, they have since all died. In 1885 the average height aud girth of the inai 
seedlings were 36 and 2 inches respeotively. In this year sowings were made in coppice 
sub-compartment 13 in lines of patches six feet apart from centre to centre, lines 
being sown alternately with deodér, oak, and kai. The seed germinated in August, 
but the percentage of suecess was only 29 6. 


82. (2) Bodidr.—In his ‘‘ Suggestions ”’ regarding this forest Sir D. Brandis 
snys:— Here considerable progress has beon mado in raising deodaér, and it may be 
said that both the syateme of sowing in patches and of transplanting from nurseries ig 
now well understood, and that in faveurable localities failures are now the exception. 
The first attempt at planting was made in 1872, when Mr. Whittall, then in charge 
of the Deoban rauge, began sowing in horizontal lines three feet apart, which were 
dug up continuously along the slope of the hill on all aspects, and sown in November 
and December of that ycar. In 1874 this system was considered to have failed, and 
nurseries were est@blished with a view of employing transplants. The first nurseries 
were established at the bottom of valleys in localities exposed to frost; the nurseries 
were not properly prepared, and these operations were failures. Nurseries on an 
improved plan and in more fuvourable localities were ‘ostablished in November and 
Docember, 1874, and they were added to in 1875. The seed was sown in November 
and December, 1874 and 1875, and the young plants were bedded out in the rains, 
A few were planted out in the rains of 1877, but planting was stopped because the 
rains ceused carly, The majority of the plant were put out in the rains of 1878 and 
appear to be doing well. In November and Docember, 1875, another system was 
commcnced. On favourable slopes patches were dug about 10 foot apart, and in each 
patch about 40 seeds were sown, and these patches have succcoded well. In‘L876 
patches were made in continuation of the former lines in the Bodiér bay, both on 


( 45) 


favournble and unfavourable slopes ; the former succeeded, ¢he latter were a fuilure. In 
1877 patches were mide on favourable slopes in the Thona, Romuwa and Bodifr 
basins, about 15 seeds being sown in each patch in November and December. 
Sheltor was given by branches 1n all exposed places early 1n spring, and the success 
has been good, due partly to the favourable rains of 1878. 

“A large area was planted up in 1878 79, the plants having beon raised in nur- 
series from seed sown 1n 1874 Upon the whole the success of tiia oporation has 
been good, but numerous plants have died, and’those which survive have not made 
much progress It 1s believed that the roots of those plants were much damaged 
in the process of transplanting.” 

In 1879 80 nbout 6,800 deod&r plants five years old were put out. It was 
quite impossible to ext: ict these old plants without damaging the roots. One plant 
with atap-root going straight dowo was dng up at a depth of four feet, the root 
breaking off at this point and leasing probably two feet moire in the ground Most 
of the plants taken up had thick roots broken off at two feet from the surface. 
It was attempted to remedy this by forming a nursery bed with a hard botiom of 
atones and gravol well beaten down like a luwn-tennis court, and over this fine earth 
was sproad toa depth of nine inches, and the young plants put out in lines three 
inches apart and in lines one foot apart In 1881 these plant and others grown in the 
usual way, both from sowings of 1878, were put out in Ginwa, the two kinds bemg 
planted in sopurate but adjoming places on the same slope, the lines were 15 feet 
apart and the plunts three feet apart in the lines. 

In July, 1884, about 450 large cypresses from the nursery were planted out in 
Plantation I of 1878, and numerous pits have been dug of late ycars amongst the 
older plantation and sown with kail seeds, pits in which deodfr had failed being also 
cleared and sown with the pine. They usually germimated well, but great damage has 
been done by phensants und other birds eating the seeds and rooting up the young 
plants The pits containing deodar have had the grass cleared around them and have 
been drained yearly since 1885.

87. (7) AfvAna,—Sowings and plantings have been carried on ina deanltory

maples, bird-cherry, spruce, silver fir, kail, and karsha. The rauns and bird-cherry

Speaking of oak coppicing, Sir D. Brandis says that it is most desirable to 
determine exactly how far the three oaks which form important constitaents in these 
forests, vic., karahu, moru and bén, have the power of reproduction by coppice shoots. 
It is not likely that it will ever be found advantageous to grow any of these onks in pure 
coppice woods ; but it is quite possible that, in case they should bo found to coppice 
freely, it might be found desirable to maintain the ground under coniferous trees 
atockad with oak coppice, and that the existence of such coppice growth would 
groatly facilitate the regenoration by self-sown seedlings of the coniferous forests. 
Broadly speaking, it is probable that the oaks have much too slow a rate of growth to 
justify their maintenance us component parts of the high forest, but it is quite possi- 
ble that oak coppice will be found exceedingly usctul to nurse and foster a young 
growth of silver fir, spruce, and deodar. 

As will beseen from what follows, tha coppicing of all three species has beon 
tried, that of ban being the most successful, whilst morn has not succeeded so well, 
doubtless owing to the dilliculty in findmg an area with a sufficient proportion of 
young trees.

97. Roads.—In 1869, with the exception of the one to Simla, there were no ronds 
nt all within the range, and a great doal of time and Jabour was expended in getting 
ubout from pluce to place A glance at the maps accompanying this plan will show 
what a great improvement has been effected in this respect by the Forest Department. 
The four kinds of lull roads are shown on the maps thus .—

Village trachs not cut—a black dotted line bordered with burnt sienna, 

During 1869-70, 21 miles of mule tracks were completed at a cost of Rs 350 per 
mile, and the road mide from Chakrata to Deoban, three miles, at a cost of Rs. 5,000, 
apd road-making has been continued ever since. 

A contribution of Rs 10 per mile is now made from the district fund towards the 
Ohakrata-ons road, a portion of which traverses the Deobau range and is the main 
thoroughfare 1n this part of Jaunsar. 

In 1883-84 the sum of Rs. 5 was paid to the siyana of Kurwa for the right of 
making a fuel-road through village-land trom the Chakr&ta cart road to the boundary 
of the Kurwa forest.

All the fire-protected portions of the Deoban range, except such outhying patches 
as Kua, Singori, Bandla and Kota, bat including Kurwa, are closed against hunting 
aud shooting from the 15th of October to the 15th of July, except with the written 
permnission uf the officer iu charge of the Juanadr Division. Special permission is, 
however, given ty all officers of the regiment stationed at Chakréta to shoot in the 
cloyed resorves, and the Coionel commanding the regiment can give any soldier 
written permission to shoot ; but the dates of all pormits, with name of holder, is kept 
in a register in the orderly-room, so that in cnse of damage by fire, or otherwise, the 
offender may be traced. 


As a check onthe destruction of bivds for the auke of their plumage, a fine of 
Re. 5 will be inflicted for oach skin brought into the cantonments of Chakifta in the 
breeding season, unless the owner proves them to have been shot at some other time. 
A list of birda to which this rule applies has been drawn up by the cantoninent 
eommittee.

Foreat gavden.—1n 1880, 48 acres were taken up for a forest garden below the 
dak bungalow and the meeting referred to drew up the following rules :— 


(1) That all fuel may be removed by the cantonment authorities acting in 
concert with the forest officer. The revenue as hitherto to remain with the canton- 
ment authorities and the forest officer to have the power of superintending the cutting 
und removal of fuel, 60 that no damage shall be done to the garden. 


In this respect 
tho fuel contractor is bound by the orders of the forest officer, 


(2) It is understood that all works, roads, buildings, &¢., may be carriod ont 
within the limits of the grant of land by the Forcat Department without reference to 
the cnntonment authorities, and that the Forest Department may cul down any trees 
that are necessory for the carrying wut of such works in the garden, 


(3) The Forest Department is authorized to make a canal for the supply of 
water, part of which canal runs outside the grant, and to foll any trees which stand 
in the way of its construction. The Forest Department has the entire charge of the 
canal and the roadway along it.

The list of rates current in the Deoban range is given in Appendix M, and the 
following considerations will show why the charges for fuel and timber at Chakrdta

108. Carriage of fuel.—In the case of fuel the unit of measurement is 100 cubic 
feat stacked. For felling, outting and stacking the rate ie Rs. 2 per 100 cubic feet 
etacked for oak and Rs. 1-12-3 for fir. There is also & per cent. to be paid to contractors, 
which comes to one anna seven pies per 100 cubic foot. For carriage the rate is 
Ra. 1-6-0 per 100 cubio feet of stacked oak per mile and Rs. 1-2-0 for fir, the mileage 
being calculated by the nearest quarter of a mile of road over which tho fuel has to 
be cartied, and there is also a 5 per cent commission to be paid on these rates if the 
contractors have given satisfaction. The carrying contract can be terminated by a 
three months’ notice given by either party in Ootober. The rate was reduced by 
two annas per 100 cubio feet stacked in 1885. 


For stacking in the shed the rate is now four annas per 100 oubic foot stacked ; 
in the opgn, three annas.

Chakrata to Dakbpathar for five annas per maund cach way, provided that 2 contract

frum Chokréta, Carriage would, be interrupted for the three winter months.

earry 10 maunds and can go and return ina week, Supposing there wns carriage

The commissariat rations for one draught bullock are sevon seors of bhusa, 10 
seers of dry fodder or 15 seers of green fodder por diem; and as the ordinary price 
of Lhusa is two maunds for the rupee, whereas in harvest time four maunds can be 
obtained, Rs. 3 per month should suffice for the keep of one bullock. 


The above allows 10 spare bullocks and five spare carts, and the cost per maund 
would be only a4 little over four and a half aunas ; but the estimate is baced on the 
supposition, which is extremely doubtful, that it would bo profitable to cart down rai 
planks or other ecantling to Dékbpathar and then carry them on the sleepor rafts to 
Delhi, each raft of 160 sleepors being capable of carrying 40 maunds, 


As this trado is so problematical, it will be best to consider that there will only be 
carriage up the hill, and in this case the cost would not be less than eight annas a 
maund. As one maund of dry bakli and gosfim fuel contains two and a half 
stacked cubic feet, the carriage of 100 stacked oubio feet to Chakréta would cost 
lis, 20, but the cost of preparation would be a little higher, so that at the present rates 
for mule carriage it would be cheaper to cart fuel up from Kalsi than to briny it on 
mules from a greater distance than 10 miles. 


The rate for charcoal at Chakrfta being four annas a enbic foot or Re, 1 
per maund, while the cost of manufacture is one anna and a half per cubic fuot or 
gix annas per maund, it would pay to bring charcoal up from this forest.

107. Charooal.—The quantity of charcoal required is not well known, as much 
of this has hithorte been supplied by other agencies, some being made hy a contractor

in the cantonment forests and some being brought from private forests near

About 1,000 cubic feet are required annually for the Military Works workshops 
at Chakrdta, 4,000 cubic feet for the Brewery, and probably between 6,000 and 10,000 
cubic feet for the station. 


Tho Secretary of State has suggested the experimental cultivation of dogwood 
in India in connection with the manufacture of gunpowder. It is possible that some 
of the Him&layan species of dogwood may be suitable for the purpose, and in this 
range two species, Cornus macrophylla and capitata, fre pretty common, being found 
scattered along moist and shady ravines between 5,000 and 8,000 feet, thus chiefly 
occurring in the zone of the ban oak List year some charcoal was made from them 
by the Divisional Officer and sent to the Kirki factory, from which no report has as 
yet been received, The charcoal was made, however, in the ordinary way, whercas 
it would appoar that it requires to be specially prepared, vide “ The Indian Forostcr” 
tor April, 1888, page 109.

When the forest demarcation was carried out certain portiuns of most of the 
khats’ old area was reserved as Government forest, and for facility of reference such 
portions were called by the name of the khat they formerly formed part of. It is 
obvious, however, that they did not necessarily form convenient forest blocks and 
gradually the whole aren got redivided into blocks of convenient size with goud natural 
boundaries, and in most cases with local names different to the khat names. Advan- 
tage was tukon in 1877 of the publication of the maps of the Deoban working circle 
to record the new sub-divisions and to show al) the blocks on the maps. It is these 
blocks which are shown in the table given above, 


114. Formation of compartmentsa.—In his Preliminary Working Plan for those 
forests Sir D. Brandis makes the following remarks regarding the formution of 
compartments:— 


“Compartments are intended as much as possible to comprise slopes of the same or 
nearly the same aspect, and forests of a similar if not uniform character requiring 
the same or nearly the same treatment. Thus a slope with a northerly aspect 
would be formed into one compartment if the forests were of nearly the same cha- 
racter throughout. But if one portion, for instance, mainly consisted of mature trees 
and the other of trees of the younger classes, then they would be divided into two or 
more compartments by natural or artificial lines, Again, if the uppor part of the slope 


( 59 ) 


consisted of oak mixed forest and the lower part of coniferous trees, then the upper 
portion would, if of sufficient extent, furm one compartment, while the lower part 
of the slope wonld, according to circumstances, form ono or several distinct com- 
partments ; but areas of small extent in which the forest has a character different 
feom the rest would not be formed into separute compartinents, 

The varied nature of the Deoban forest will, in many cases, make it impos- 
sible to form compartments uniforra in churacter aa regards present forest growth ; 
in such cases, therefore, their boundaries will be determined by the configuration of 
the ground and by convenient boundary lines, such as roads, ravines, ridges and the 
like. Henoe it will bo evident that in working these forests we must deal not 
so much with compartments as with portions and sub-divisions of compartments, or 


sub-compartments as we may call them.” 

These remarks have been remembered in the formation of the present compart- 
ments, but in many cases where the slopes are short, forests with different aspects 
nave had to be included in one compartment to avoid the evil of too minute a 
subdivision, and vatnral features or roads have invariably been used as boundaries, 
undefined artificial boundaries being in no instance employed, except in one or 
two compartments at Kalsi, as most of the blocks are as yet not fully opened out by 
paths, and when they come to he worked, paths can be made in such a way us to 
separute two marked types of forest, so that, when this plan comes to be revised, tho 
compartments oan be reformed accordingly : but to do this at once for the whole of 
the furests would bo impracticable. 

The present compartments vary in extent usually between 60 and 250 acres, 
and are all shown on the accompanying maps, whilst the areas under different types 
of forest in each of them are givon in Appendix D. 

115. Former valuation surveys.—In 1865 some valuation surveys were made in 
this forest by the Inspector-General, vide his Bashabr report of that year. In 1877-79, 
the Mohna, Kanjatra, Thona, Kopti, Asméri and Kutniae blocks were divided into 
compartments by Mr. Dansay and a detailed description of each compartment was 
written. An enumeration survey of Molina was also completed, but no records can 
now be found of this work. 1t is believed, however, that the division was considered 
much too minute, some of the compartments being only 10 acres in extent. 


In 1879-80 a valuation survey of the Hajawa block was made by Mr. Murray, as 
recorded in the annual report for that year.

CHAPTER VIII. 


THE YIELD OF THE FORESTS. 


117. Perhaps the most important question in connection with these forests is the 
determination of their annual possible yield, as until this has heen done with a fair 
degree of accuracy it is impossible to fix the area necessary for the permanent 
supply of Obukréta with fuel and timber. This problem is rendered very complicated 
by the extrome irregalarity of the forests and the varying rates of growth of the numer- 
ous component species, and also of the same species at different aspects and ultitude. 
This yield is not, therefure, determinable until further observations have been made, 
but the demands fer projnace ure limited and much less than even the present 
capabilities of the forests included in the runge. 


In the Preliminary Working Plan previously referred to it is clearly shown 
that the only satisfactory unit by which this yield can be expressed is the cubic foot 


of solid wood.

Reducing factor. 

119. The measures to determine this factor were taken in accordance with the 

directions of the preliminary working plan, which were as follows :— 


The contents of a tree, including its branches, may be regarded as a portion of 
a cylinder the baso of which is the base of the tree and the longth is its height. 
The co-efficient or fraction, which, multiplied with the cylinder, gives the cubic con- 
tents of the trec, we have agreed to call the reducing factor. Itis evident that the 
yalue of this reducing factor varies with the shapo of the tree and its branches, and 
that certain classes should be established for each of which a reducing factor must 
be determined. This is a large tusk, which requires long and *systematic research, 
and entails the analysis of numerous sample trees. In tho present case we will limit 
our researches to the determination of 2 mean reducing factor for each kind of trea 
with a girth exceeding 4 feet 6 inchea. Sucho mean factor being determined, all 
that is required for determining the cubic contents of the trecs in one compartment 
is to multiply the aggregate sectional area of oach height of class with the average 
height of the treos in that class, and to sum up tho results. It is obviously immate- 
rial at what height from the ground the sectional area of the base is taken, provided 
it is taken always at the same height. For the Jaunsar Forests I would suggest 


5 fect. 
16pD 


Tho value of the mean reducing factor must be ascertained by the analysis ofa 
number of snmple trees of different heights and girths above 4 feet 6 inches. The 


procedure will be as follows :— 


lat,—Age and rate of growth to be ascertained by counting the rings ona 


mean radius of the stumps from 2 to 2 inghes. 


2nd,—Girth at 5 feet from the ground, and total height to be measured. 


3rd,—Cuhic contents to be determined by actual measur: ment in the following 
manner :—The stem to be lugged, all symmetrical pieces to be measured, and their 
eoutents to be calculated as cylinders by their moan diameter, the cubic contents of 
all irregular pieces and small wood, as far us saleabldé, to bu determined by stacking. 


From the measurements whicl have since boan made, full particulars of which 
aro given in appendix Q, the following monn reducing factors have been deduced :—

Relation between stacked fuel an ite solid contente, 


120. In 1879 an experiment to determine this relation was carried out at 
Chakrata by Mr. Smythies, but owing to difficulty about the sypply of water, it camo 
to a premature conclusion, and for tho same reason it bas not been continued since. 


A tank 84 feet decp and about 6§ feet square was built of solid masonry, and 
an escape hole for the water at the bottom wus constructed out of a large bamboo. 
The inside of the tank was finished with smooth plaster. Two casks furnished with 
taps were placed on a stand at one side of the tank, and wator was,supplied to the 
easks by means of a small canal, being the waste water of the Chakréta water-works. 
Each cask was measured to hold 54 gallons, and each was supplied with a rod 
graduated to every 10 gallons (one of them to every 5 gallons), An imporial gallon 
measure was issued to gauge the casks, and the graduation of the rods was twice 


tested carefully. 


The ompty tank was first of all measured by means of the casks, and it was found 
that up to a certain mark, which was indicated on all the four walls when the water 


was level, the tank held 986 gallons. 


The fuel operated on was fir stocked in lines of 300 cubic feet each ; it was 
placed, a cortain amount at a timo, in three -parallel lines, and was kept down by 
fixed cross-beams. The three inoveable scantlings were partially immersed in the 
water every timo, but this was ullowed for when the tank was measured, ie., thay 
floated at the top of the water underneath the cross-beam in the same reapective 
positiona they held afterwards. The fuel being carefully stacked in the tank, water 
was admitted, a cask at a time, and when the water camo up tothe mark on tho 
walls, the amount was recorded ; it generally happening that eight full casks and a 


fow gallons were required each time. 


The rosult of the experiment on 1,200 cubic feet stacked was found to he that 
in stacked fuel about two-thirds the amount ts soltd wood, ie, 100 cubie feet of solid 


yood==150 stacked cubic feet of fuol. 


Neither the casks nor the tank leaked, and the chiof source of error Inid in the 
absorption of wator by the fuel, which was two and-a-half years old and exceedingly 


dry.

131. Thinnings—In the treatmont of this working circle, the groat object 
should be the production of deodar, and as this species hus some formidable rivals to 
contend ayainst, notably the firs in the Karamba block aod kail in parts of Konaia, it 
muat be aided in its strugyle by thinnings. In the Karamba and Rikta blocks this 
aid will consist of the first three operations mentioned in paragraph 80 of the Mundéli 
plan, tuking care to give the young deodér saplings and poles plenty of light by 
entirely uncovering tho leaf-canopy overhead, especially in Karamba, where there is 
usually a dense fir forest mixed with deoddr. The trees will have to be killed by 
girdling, since the distance of Karamba prohibita the wood being carried to Chakraéta 
for fuel, whilst ia Kikts, although the distance is somewhat less, there are no fuel 
rouds, and it would scarcely pay to make them fur the small quantity of fuel ayail- 
able, but good first class rai fit for timber should, as a rule, be spared. In Konain, 
on the contrury, it will be advisable to make improvement fellings, on the same prin- 
ciple as the girdling operations above in compartments 4, 6 and 7, the fuel being 
carried to Chakrata. The fellings should begin in Konaia 4 in 1880 and continue 
until the three compartments have been worked over. It is true that the distance js 
114 miles or 1} miles beyond the usual limit of fael operations, but it will be better 
toincur the extra cost of carriage than to allow so muny dry trees to remain standing 
in the first class forest, thus increasing the risks from fires and inseots, Young kail, 
&o., which are too small to be cut up into fuel, will simply be girdled or cut down, and 
no fuel will be exported from compurtments 3, 6 and 8, which are more remote. 


As lime is often burnt for departmental use in compartment 7 Konain, no 
oaks should be girdled or cut for fuel for Chukréta in this block, but they may be 
felled from time to time as required for the supply of fuel to the kilns,

IlL—THE LOKANDI CIRCLE.

136. Thinnings amongst young deoddy.—In compartments 1, 2,4, and 5 of 
Bodiar, compartment 4 of Kdpti, and in Kandsar there are some dense patches of 
almost pure deodér saplings and poles which it would be beueficial tothin out, great 
care boing taken to preserve the more healthy and vigorous stems, and not to isolate 
them, but to maintain the leaf-canopy sufficiently close to enable them to be drawn 
up and to acquire long clean boles. In this operation all suppressed trees which no 
longer serve any useful purpose may be extracted. The work is not, howover, 
urgent and should only be undertaken when a sale can be*found fur the produce at 
Chakréta or to supply indents from the Sappers and Miners, Koorkeo, or others. 

The main operations to be carried out during tho term of this plan must have 
for their sole object the improvement of the working circle as a deodir-producing 
area and will be two in numbor : —

136 (a). Improvemeut fellings and cleanings.—Owing to their distance from 
Chakréta, no improvement fellings can be made in the Inéri and Moila blocks, as the 
produce could not be utilised and girdling is not very neceasary. Elsewhere in the 
circle fuel can be brought out, as although in some compartments the distance is a 
little over ten miles, yet it is preferable to incur the extra expense, rather than to 
allow a large numbor of dead and girdled trees to remain standing in the foresta, 
Where, however, the distance is over ten miles as in the Bodiér bleck and in comn- 
parments 1 and 2 of Missau, only such trees noed be felled ns are actually damaging 
young deodér or are certain to prove injurious to them within the next 20 years; 
whereas elsewhere the fellings should be conducted on a diffurent system, trees being 
marked more freely and besides telieving all deodir seedlings, saplinga and poles 
from suppression; all trees helping to contract the crowns of large deoddr should be 
felled, and large gaps should be made in the leaf-canopy near seed-bearing deodar 
wherever it is reasonable to expect reproduction of that species, the great object boing 
to increase the number of deod&r at the expense of the inferior species. In these 
fellings trees of all sizes should be cut, down to the smallest poles, where such are 
suppressing or are likely to suppress young deod&r ; but where there are no deodar, 
fellings should not be made unless the place is within ten miles of Chakr&ta; and in 
this caso only trees over well-established advance growth'should be cut, save in com- 
partments 1 and 2, Kopti and in Kundsar, which are the only arcas closed to grazing. 


Tt will be noticed what a large numbor of the first class deoddér are worthless 
for timber. 1n cases where such trees are found growing amongst a full crop of 
young deodar, and are no longer required for seeding purposes, they should be cut 
out in the course of the ordinary improvemont fellings, but being inferior’ to fir as fuel 
not more than 10,000 oubiec feet should be cut out in any one year. Any portions 
of such trees which may be fit for timber should on no account be cut up into fuel, 
but either used as timber for departmental purposes, or sold or given away as a part 
of the free grant to the villagers.

On those three different conditions must mainly depend tho system on which the 
forests should be worked ; thus, in arenas closed to fires and grazing, trees can be 
felled freely, and any method of working suitable to a hilly country can be adopted 
in tho areus closed to fires but open to grazing, trees should only be felled in places 
where the advance growtb is abundant, established, and out of danger from grazing 
animals ; in areas open to fires, dry and fullen trees alune should be taken oat, 


The first-named areas are not sufficient to meet permanently tho Commissariat 
demands unless supplemented by the others; aud if the station is enlarged it muy be 
necessary to add other aroas more remote from Ohakr&ta to the working circle, The 
Bhujkoti, Gutug&d, Sidri, Duliana and Inéri blocks might thus have to be included 
which would be an addition of 1,580 acres of well-stucked forest, 674 acres containing 
oak, 792 fir, and 61 deodar, as the predominating species. <A certain quantity of fue 
will ulso always be obtuinable from the Lokandi circle. 


140, Fire-protection and grazing -grounds.—The first great improvement called 
for is the protection of the remaining forests in the working circle from fire, and this 
is the more important as they coutuin large quantities of oak, our most valuable fuel, 
although at present there are not quite 500 acres of ouk forest protected from fires and 
grazing. As can be seen from the description of compartinents, it is not worth while 
treating as forest Marauwa 3, Daroa 4, Ekra 3, Kalerod 4, Amldékri 3, and Leguna 1, 
nggrogating 882 acres, and these should be so: aside as permanent grazing srens. 
Marauwa 5, Hajawa 5, the Ekra, Kulered and Asldékri blocks, containing 1,094 aores of 
oxk foroat, should be brought under fire-protection as soon xs possible, tho fire-linus 
being made so ns to exclude the extensive areas of grassy and rocky slopes in the 
Kkra and Kulered blocka, which it would be superfluous tu protect and which are 
valued as pasture grounds.

The use of the saw in folling troos in this circle should be insisted on except 
perhaps in the case of large and knotty old onksa. The fuel contractors are in the 
habit of folling the trees rather too high above the ground, and although a consider- 
able improvement has tuken place of late years, this tendency must be checked and a 


( 73 ) 


fine shoald bo imposed on the contmeotor for every tree improperly felled. The bill- 
men are also accustomed to leave knotty or partially hollow logs unsawn, thus causing 
a waste of material and giving the coupe an untidy appearance. This the ranger 
should be instructed to rectify and see that all pisces fit tor fuel are properly utilised.

IV.—THE KARSHU COPPIOE, 


147. A coppice of ban onk hus already baon started at Kurwa, and it is pro- 
posed to form a similar one for kurshu of ubout the same area and on the eame rota- 
tion, twonty years. The best placo for such a coppice would seem to be the uppor 
portion of the Gedi khad, Hujiwa 8, where there is a dense growth of kurshu saplings 
and poles extending right up to the top of the Tipra tiba. Tho area, 48 acros, is not, 
however, quite lurge cnough tor our purposo, and it will be better to add to it 12 acres 
in Jédi 9, which can be coppiced after the Hajawa portion has been finished. The 
rvtation is quite an arbitrary one, as there are at present no data for determining the 
proper period. The area in Hnjawa should be divided into three-acre plots as has 
ulready been done at Kurwa, and theso plots should be numbered in the order in which 


( “4 ) 


they are to be coppiced. The coppicing sheuld take place in November and Decem- 
ber before the snow falls and from fifteen to twenty trees per acre should be left as 
standards The stundards will never be of any use as timber, and the only object 
in reserving them is that in converting a high forest into coppice it is important to 
keep some seed-bearers to fill up the gaps which are certain to be formed by a number 
of tho older trees failing to coppice properly. Stumps over one foot in diameter need 
not bo drossed, as larger ones do not, as ua rule, send out good coppice shoots. The 
result of the coppicing should be carefully watched and acorns sown if necessary in 
the blanks. 

When the area has been preperly divided up a map should be prepared on the 
scale of 20 inches=1 mile, showing all tbe divisions, and on this map particulars of the 
fellings can be subsequently entered year by year. 

The yield from the coppice will probably be about 2,000 stacked oubio feet of 
fuel per ucre and will form a part of Oummissariat fuel supply.

than to carry it on mules’ backs, 


151. Treatment.—The coppicing should be dono, as hitherto, in December and 
January, one sub-compartment being coppiced euch year in the order in which they 
are numbered on the accompanying tracing. From ten to twenty standards should 
be left per acre, and no ayir stumps need be dressed, as the species is such a worthless 
one, whilst only stumps one foot in diameter or less of other species require dregsing s 
the other ones, not, as a rule, sending out coppice shoots, The result of the coppicing 
operations should be carefully watched and blanks filled up by sowing ban acorns 
or pine seeds. 

After all tho sub-compartments in Kurwa 2 have been coppiced it may be found 
that the rotation is too short, vide para. 93, and that the first areas are not yet ready 
for re-coppicing. In this case a portion of Bandla should be coppiced and protected 
from grazing, but it will not probably be necessary to close more than 20 acres. 

Kurwa 1 should be coppiced and planted up within the next three years in 
accordance with tho plantation scheme.

The cnumoration surveys show, vide para. 161, that no more dry wood remains in 
the blocks valued, and this is probably the case alsoin the romaining forests: so that 
the revenue under the first item will in futuro be almost ntl unless it be determined 
to fell green trees, and this, if it is intended to keep theso areas permanently undor 
forest, should only be done where domestic animals are excluded, as grazing ig parti- 
cnlarly injurious amongst oaks, 


( 7 ) 


It will be necessary, therefore, to modify the grazing contract on its expiration 
in 1892, and to exclude from the areas open to grazing the Chilmeri, Patiadhar, and 
Gobréni blocks, with the exception of 240 aores, viz., compartment 4, Chilmeri, and 
compartments 2 and 4, Patiadhér, which are open grassy blanks dotted with a few 
trees only, save a fringe along the larger nalas. It has been considered necessary 
to include these grass lands in the areas to be leased for purposes of control, as thoy 
run right through the Ohilmeri and Patiadbér blooks, and it may also be neceasary 
to make paths through them for the export of fuel from the neighbouring compart. 
ments, but grasing need in no way be interfered with. The now area which should 
be closed to grazing in 1892 amounts to 967 acres, so that the total area closed in 
Cantonments will then be 1,493 dores. Grass-cutting can, however, be allowed, and 
these blocks will be very valuable for this purpose, for all horbaceons growth in the 
open parts being cropped close by cattle, very little grass will be obtainable elsewhere. 
Excluding building sites, &., there will remain over 2,000 acres of grass land and 
oak forest available for grazing purposes. Presuming that Kewal Ram receives dues 
on 600 animals which would give him a handsome profit on his lease, there would 
still, if these proposals be adopted, be more than three acres available for every 
animal, and this should more than suffice, since the average throughout Jaunsar is 
only one and a halfacresa head. It must also be recollected that there are over 1,000 
aeres of open (iovernment forest within a fow miles of Obakr&ta, where oows, &co., 
required for the milk supply could graze. 


No more land must be broken up for cultivation in the forest blocks and at the 
end of the present lease all large patches of cultivation which it is advisable to main- 
tain as such should be maiked off by pillars, and where the boundary is an artificial 
one a two-foot line should be dug from pillar to pillar. Small patchos, on the contrary, 
which it ie dificult to mark off, and which twist in and out amongst forest growth, 
should be abandoned and planted up. This work should be carried out by the Forest 
officer in consultation with the Oantonment Magistrate, and will not probably affect 
the Cantonment Funds adversely to the extent of more than Hs. 36 per annum. 


As the supply of dry wood has been exhausted and it would be inadvisable to 
fell green trées on no systén whatever, these proposals will have the effect of giving 
to the Cantonment Fund a permanent annual revenue of Ks. 500, whilst the services 
of one of the two Cantonment forest guards could be dispensed with, thus effecting a 
gaving in expenditure of Ke. 84. It is probable, with the inorease in the size of the 
station, that the value of the grazing lease will be considerably enhanced in spite of 
the additional areas suggested for closing, but supposing that it is renewed at its 
present rate only, the totul forest revenue in 1892 is estimated as follows :—

166. Order of cutting.—In onse the forests are leased it will be best to work

seedlings, such spots must be immediately planted up. 


167. Bfanagement of the Foreat Garden,—The small area stocked with oak in

as have done best in the garden. The following kinds are recommended :—Pinus kha- 
gia, Sinenaia, maritima, sylvestris and luricio and eryptomeria japonica. It would be 
aut of place here to enter into details about the treatment of the fruit trees and 
vegetables.

169. Area to be treated as stored eoppice.—There remains on area of 1,094 acres to

GENERAL WORKS. 
IMPROVEMENT OF THE BOUNDARIES. 


174. Where the outer boundary does not follow a stream or a rood it is usually 
marked off by a line of pillars, but owing to the complicated configuration of the 
g ound ore pillar is often uot visible from the next, or it becomes hidden after a 
timu by a dense growth of brushwood, and thero is often in consequence uncertainty 
as to how the boundary runs in many places. ‘Tho remedy is to cut a two-foot 
path from pillar to pillar wherever the boundary is not a natural one or o 
road does not alroady exist. Such a path has just been made from pilar 84, 
at Morrow’s Neck to pillar 5 to the west of the Biti gad, and it should be con- 
tinued all round these foresta from west to east until pillar 34 is again reached. 
Then the outlying onk forests should bo taken in hand, and finally that portion of the 
Kalsi forest which ts to be worked. In para. 6 it is estimated that there are 73 miles 
of artificial boundary, and seven m:ies should be made every year, so that after ton 
or eleven years the whole work will have been completed. 


( 88 ) 


It will then probably be found thet there are an unnecessary number of outer 
boundary pillars and a hundred pillars should be inspeoted each year, the superfluous 
ones being removed and the others renumbered. This will take two or three years 
more, go that in from twelve to fourteen years the boundary will be in perfect order 
and defined beyond the possibility of dispute. The cut boundary line will prove of 
great service as un inspection path, can be utilised as a fire line, and if necessary 
could in places be enlarged into a fuel path, 

Tho boundaries of the chaks which were demarcated last year are being defined 
in a similar manner to that recommended in the case of the outer boundary, and this 
work should be completed if possible within the year. After completion thay should 
all be inspected! to see that their boundaries have been laid down in accordance with 
the temporary demarcation made by myself.

In addition to these planting operations Rs. 200 will have to be spent annually 
on the same work in the Cantonment blocks if these are leased, and all the old plan-

It must be borne in mind that the table givon above is not intended to bo rigorously 
adhered to, as this would be impossible owing to the fact that seed is not always 
procurable. It should, however, be worked up to as far as possible, and in case the 
work falls into arrears every effort should be made in a good seed year to recovor 
lost ground, and large nurseries should then be established in order to utilise as 
much as possible of the seed. It may be thought that Bodiér should be planted up 
more rapidly, but financial considerations and the numerous planting works in other 
pacts of the division render this inadviauble. 


( 84 ) 


Sites for deodar aurseries should be chosen in well-drained localities not exposed 
to severe frosts as aro the low hollows in Bodifr; the dol should be good but 
not stiff, and there should be a certain amount of Jateral protection on the south. 
The nursery beds should be terraced and made about four feet wide. The soil 
shoald be mixed with some good moald and the seeds sown in lines a foot apart im 
November and December. Germination takes place in March and April, and in the 
following July the young secdlings should be bedded out three inches apart in the 
lines, where they should remam for two or at tho outside three years and should then 
be planted out in the forest. Whilst in the nursery the young plants should be 
protected in seasons of drought and frost by moans of grass tatties raised a few 
feet off the ground, these being removed when snow falls, and an occasional watering 
in dry weather, if water is handy, wou!d be very beneficial. 

The young deoddr should be put out on the blank slopes immediately the rains 
begin in horizontal lines of pits 5” x 5,’ the pits being dug 2’ long x 1’ » 1’ and 
filled with good mould where the soil is poor and stony, or such soil should at least be 
sifted with the hand and freed from stones. it will be advisable to prepare these pita 
before the rains break, so as not to lose a moment in filling them up with trans- 
plants, as it is of the first importance that all the plants should be wel established 
when the monsoon ends, to enable them to withstand the drought of November and 
the subsequent frosts, and they should be protected at such seasons by means of grass 
cowls, which will not cost much, for if this precaution be naglected the success of 
the plantation will be endangered. « 

In sowings deodfr should be sown after the seed is collected in November, but 
kail, chir, and rai should be kept and not sown until the rains, as it is only then 
that they germinate, and if sown before thay are liable to be eaten by birda, mice, da, ; 
in fact even in the rains it will often be necessary to protect them from such 
ravages by covering tho pits with thorns, &o.,.and at Bodidr, where pheasants are 
eommon, it may even be advisable to plant kuil and not ¢u sow it. 


Hitherto in a mixed plantation of deodaér and kail jt has been customary to have 
one row of deodfér and one of kail, or those species in alternate pits. it is found, 
however, that in this way the knil affords dittle or no shelter to the young deodir and 
in future they should both be grown in the sume pit, the young kail being cut away 
when no longer required as nurses, leaving about five por cent. to form part of the 
mature crop and to become the future seed-bearers. All the pits must be carefally 
drained, otherwise the plants remain yollow and grow slowly. Kail scad can usually 
be obtained in large quantities every year, and it will therefore be better at Bodidr 
and Konain first te prepare che pits ard eow them up with kajl in July, filling up 
failures next year with transplants from the nursery where a stock of young kail and 
deod&r should be kept. When sufficient deoddr seed can be obtained the pits con- 
taining the young kail which germinated in July can be resewn on the sheltered 
wide with deod4r in the sucoeeding November, and blanks can be subsequently filled 
up by planting out two-year-old deodaér from tho nursery on the sheltered side ef the 
kail and in the same pit. It is for this reason that tho pits have to be made two feet 
long instead of the customury length of one and a half foot. In sowings generally 
enly about ten seeds should be put im each pit and they should be separated as far as 
possible from each other and not putin all tagether. This is a necessary caution, as in 
several old sowings there wasa lavish expenditure of seed, the young plants coming 
up in a buoch to the detriment of each other. Tho kail seeds had better be sown in 
the exposed half of eaoh pit, so that the young plants may the more efficiently protect 
the deodar by which they are eventually to be replaced. In the raius the grass, if 
luxuriant, should be cleared immediately around the young plants. 


In some of the rockier parts of Bodiér cypress might with advantage be planted 


out. 
In tho fuel plantations the same general remarks apply, but the pits need only be 
one and a half foot long. Although our experiments with Australian trees have been 


( 8 ) 


ao far o failure, I would yet recommend that we give them a farther trial over a 
couple of acres at Kurwa, every precaution being taken to ensure success. Acacia 
deourrens, which is capable of withstanding long droughts and grows on exposed 
slopes and on any soil, might be tried, as wellas other Australian species which 
have succeeded well at Ranikhet, whence seed might be obtained, as that produced 
there has proved to be fertile. The seedlimgs should bo planted in Hines four feet 


apart. 


Io Matkdéngra Sir D. Brandis recommended that a cypress nursery should be 
established, and an attempt made to plant out this species on rocky slopes having a 
south-easterly aspect, strong plattts being used in ordor to ensure success ; but sow- 
ings of cypress in situ are not advisable. Moru should only be sown in moist 
localities at cool aspects, ban on the more exposed slopes, and about six acorna per 
pit should suffice.

It will bo observed from the above scheme that it is contemplated returning to 
the same compartments and renewing the operations after an interval of ten years in 
tho caso of the kail areas, and of fifteen years in the caso of the fir and onk urens. 
The cleanings and thinnings must therefore be cariied out in such a manner that 
their beneficial effects last for these periods. The kail which grows so much 
fuster than the deodair should be cut out or girdled freely, leaving however a sufficiency 
of pine to afford lateral protection to the young deoddr, but destroying all those at 
preeent overtopping the more valuable species or which are likely to prove injurious 
to them before tho end of the ten year period. It would probably be better to do the 
cleaning iu fivo years instead of ten and to return evory five years over the same 
ground, and tho programme should be modified acvordingly if it be found possible to 
procure the additional supervision aod Jabor necessary to carry this out. Wherever 
possible the kuil cut down or girdled should be utilised as fuel for Chnkraéta and not 
left in the forest, where the presence of many dry saplings and poles will cause fires to 
to be dreaded and will encourage insects. 


The thinning in the fir and oak forests shonld be conducted according to the 
rulos laid down by Mr. Fernandez in the Mund4li plan, omitting rule No. 1V., and 
the young deodir should be completely freed from all cover overhead, all trees Leing 
girdled or cut down which at present overtop them or are likely to do so within the 
next fifteen yoars,

trunsport of timber and fuel to Chakrata The timber will have to be carried us 
hithorto on men’s backs and only about five mules of timber-carrying paths will have

Commissariat will be taken from blocks along the great Deoban ridge, if we except

term of this plan. All the fuel will have therefore to pass by Deoban, and it is from

179. Wire trameay not feasible. —It has been suggested that a wire tramway 
might be preferable to a sledge road, but this is out of the question on the ground of 
expense, even if it were otherwise suitable to the placo. The proposed wire tramway 
from Naini Tal to Katgodam is estimated to cost nearly Rs. 20,000 a mile and 
repairs Rs. 2,000 per mile, so that oven if one could be made from Deoban to Cuakréta 
for half this sum tho cost would be altogether prohibitive. 


180. Construction of wheeled tramway to be considered.—When the sledge road 
has been completed and isin working order, it may be considered whether it would 
not pay to make a wheeled tramway in continuation of it as far as the Parado fuel- 
shed, where about hulf the fuel will have to be taken, or whether it would not be 
cheaper to carry the fuel there by means of carts with low wheels made expressly 
for the purpose, rather than on mules’ backs. 


Tho lower Mobna road to Kandal was originally laid ont ata fairly even gradient 
with the idew of laying down a tramwny on it, und it has been thought that it might 


{ 88 ) 


be possible toextend this to Panddwa with a break only at Chanddri Idni and thus to 
tap all the eastern portion of the range. A one-foot line was accordingly laid down 
last year, but the Duliéna cliff proved an insurmountable obstacle, and the project had 
to be abandoned The one-foot line, however, where it runs through Hajéwa 7 can 
be widened and utilised as a fuel road when the timo comes to work this compartment. 


181. Fuel pathe.—Nearly 60 miles of fuel paths will be necessary to open out 
all the compurtmenta proscribed fur working during tho next 20 years, or about three 
miles a year. Hitherto such paths have been madein rather a hbnap-hazard sort of 
way, and there have been inatances in which the fuel has beon first cnt and stacked 
and paths made subsequently up to the stacks. It is most important, however, that 
these paths should be properly laid out, as it is often possible to save a mile or so of 
carriage and thus bring the forests so mach nearer the market. Whenever, therefore, 
a compartment is about to be worked for the first time, a regolar plaw should be 
drawn up, showing the best method of laying ont the roads and the nearest line of 
export to Chakrdta. Take, as an example, compartment 5, Missau. The export 
might be made at present eithor vid Koti, Kandsar, and Veoban, vid Lokandi ldni and 
Deoban or vid Lokandi lini and Jadi. On working out the distances, howevor, it is 
found that the shortest route of the three is the one uid Koti, Kanfsar, and Deob&n, 
and this would of course be taken were it not that by inaking a path from the 
Missau road close to the Missau néla and through the Asmari bluck to Thona léni we 
can shorten the distance by nearly a mile, and by means of this new path can also 
tap Missau 4 and two compartments of Asméri. Tho distance from Lokandi lfni to 
Chakr&ta vié eoban is a mile and a half less than that vid Jédi, and the latter road 
ehould therefore only be used for produce coming from the lower parts of the 
Kutnifr and Jadi blocks, from Darna and from most of Matkéngra. 


182. Present method of calculating distances from Chakrdta to be changed. —Now 
that all the blocks have been divided into compartments of fairly small size it will 
be better to pay for the carriage of fuel and timber according to the mean distance 
of each compartment from the forest chauki at Chakréta instead of measuring up 
the exact distance of euch stack of fuel or vach lot of karris as tho case may be. 
In this way much time and labor will be saved, whilst it will do away with one very 
feasible method of swindling by the addition of a quarter a mile or so to the real 
distance. A table showing the mean distance of every compartment from the chauk 
is given in Appendix N., but the figures there given are liable to correction, as paths 
iu many instances have not yet been made. They should only, however, be altered 
with the sanction of the divisional officer, who should initial the necessary corrections 
in the appendix. When fuel is carried to the Parade ground shed an addition of a 
mile and a half must be made to the figures given for all blocks north of Chakrats, 
and a similar amount deducted for blucks south of the station.

ReauLaTIoN oF Forest PriviLEces. 


184. (1) Timber.—Hitherto free-grant trees have been marked in an irregular 
taanner, the same khat often taking trees from different localities, and the villagers 
frequently being allowed to select their own trees regardless of the well-being of the 
forest. This must cease, and all troes should be marked by the divisional officer 
or the ranger as much as possible compartment by compartment according to the 
usual method of making selection fellings, no trees being marked under six feet in 
girth unless for a partioular purpose at the oxpress desire of the villagors. 


Appendix I. gives a list of forest blocks from which those freo-granta are to he 
made in the future and the names of the khats which they are intended to supply. 
In the case of deodaér tho list includes free-grants for the whole of J nuns&r, 
but for the other trees only free-grants from the forest blocks in the Deoban range 
ara given. It is possible that certain forests such as Bomnai ani Itikndér, which have 
not been valued, have an insufficient number of Inrge trees to supply the khats for 
which they have been set aside, and in this case the scheme will have to bo modified, 
but no trees shonld on any account be given away frvo or sold from the threo I. 
class forests of Bodiér, Kandsar and Konain, nor from Karimba and Rikta, except 
the twelve trecs given in the list, as the two latter blucks are reserved for the deodér 


supply of Chakréata. 


In case any khat does not take out its full number of treesin any one year it 
may bo allowed, at the discretion of the divisional officer, and as a concession not as 
a right, to extract the balance in the year following. In case a khat atill requires 
more trees, the rules in para. 28 may be carried out, each khat getting its trees from 
the forests set asido for it with the exception of the Lokandi Workin Circle, where 
only one extra tree per khat should be sold, and of the Karamba Working Circle, 


( 80 ) 


where none at all should be sold, and the khats which get their annual grants from 
these forests must obtain their extra trees from more remote forests, such as Bomnai 
Riknér, Majbira, and okie in the Bawar range. ; 


The siyAnas of each khat are accustomed to apply for their free-grant permits in 
the months of April and May. It should now be arranged that the Siyduas of khats 
who get their trees from the same forests should apply at the same time, so that one 
date may be fixed for marking their trees which will save a great deul of the ranger’s 
time, ¢g., the sivinas of khats Bumtér, Birmau, Dwar, and Bangéon will have, say, 
the 25th of the May fixed as the date on which their troes will be marked, ag they 
all get their trees from tha same forest blocks, via, Gutugid and Bhujkoti. They 
should be present on that date in the forest and kccompuny the ranyer when he 
marks their trees. In this way uli the free-grant trees in the Deobdn range could 
probably be marked in a fortnight. 


A amall wooden chaukiat Lokandi lani wonld be useful for checking the export 
of free grantees’ timber from the forests of the Lokandi Cirele. 


A fine of two annas apicce is to be levied on all scantling shewn out with 
the axe. 


185. (2) Fuel.— Requires no regulation for the present. 


16. (3) Grazing. —The present arrangements will probably only require some 
slight modifications during the next twenty years. The isolated Raura block of 106 
acres, which it is proposed to work regulurly overy year to supply the rifle range 
with fuel, will have to be closed or fuel supplied from elsewhere at much greater 
expense. Grass-cutting, however, can be permitted, and as the rest of the khat is all 
burnt this block should form a most valuable fodder reserve for the use of the 
villagers. The portion below the path from Raura to Mairawana, or about ten acres, 
and which contains chiefly young chir, may however be left open, as it need not be 
worked, and it is inexpedient to close the path which ia much used, but if it be 
found necessary a wire-fenve will have to be run alung its upper side to prevent cattle 
straying. 


It will also be expedient to close tho small areca of karsha onk forest in the 
Pandéwa block which is to supply fuel for lime-burning and the 48 vores of Hajiwa 
8, which it is proposed to coppice, as this system is quite incompatible with grazing, 
at lenst in the younger stages of the coppice growth. These two pieces adjoin each 
other and the nearest villaye, Khatwa ix three miles away and more than two thousand 
feet below them. Itis essential to define the limits between the open and closed 
nreas and cut grazing puths two feet broad will have tobe dug around the lower 
boundary of both piccos; in the oase of Pandawa the newly cut level line might be 
used in places, and iu Hujawa 8 the fuel path leading to the Gedi khad would form 
the boundary for a part of the distance. 


If the KAlsi forest be trentod on the coppice with standard aystem (as proposed 
in para. 169) it will be necessary to alternately close and open the different compart- 
meuts coppiced, not more than 270 acres, or less than one-sevonth of the whole forest 
area neur Kalai being closed at a time. As svon therefore as these 270 acres are closed 
the 80 acres now closed in Barabigh should be reopened and ouly closed again when 
its turn for coppicing arrives. 


The proposals therefore are to close (1) 100 acres of bin onk forest at Baura ; (2) 
120 acres of karsha oak forest near Panddwe ; (3) 270 acres ut Kélsi as explained in 
para. 169, and it may aleo be necessary to close a portion of Bandla if the coppicing 
operations now carried on at Kurwa are extended to that block. These proposals are 
very moderate and should be considered quite apart from each other, as they affect 
the privileges of separate khats, Pandéwa boing 15 miles from Raura and Baus 27 
miles from Kalsi,

G. O. No. nae dated Ist September, 1887, sanctioned 2 limit being put to the 
number of cattle entitled to free grazing, and fixed it at 10 por ecnt. on the actual 
number of each kind at present found in each khat. The numbers that actually 
graze in the forest are given in Appendix J., and it is proposed to add 10 por cent. 
to these und not to inolude alarge number of goats, &c., which never enter Govern- 
ment forests, apd whose owners do not even claim the right to graze in these 
forests. The villages who do not and never have exercised their grazing rights aro 
mostly situated in the lower khats along the Jumna and the Tons far away from 
Government forests, and as the number of animals entitled to free grazing is now to 
be definitely and permanently fixed, it would seem impolitic to overburden the forests 
with unnecessary privileges, and the Conservator might ask the Local Government 
to snuction the exclusion of such cattle, sheep, and goats as never graze in the foreats 
from the liet of those entitled to tree grazing and to have the Wajib-al-arz modified 
accordingly. It may be mentioned that if all the animals entitled to free grazing 
according to the present Wajib-ul-arz in some of the outlying forests wore to fully 
exercise thoir privileges, there would be scarcely standing room for them and the 
forests, as such, would be worthless. 

187. (4) Lopping.—The rules must be more strictly enforced nnd at the end of 
the winter, which is the chief lopping season, the range officer should visit all the 
chief oak furests which are most likely to suffer, vis., Singori, Kua, Ruura, Kota, 
Bandla, Darna, Jédi, Kutniér, KEkra 2 and Kulered 8. In ouse of persistent disra- 
gard of the rules the divisional officer should withdraw his permission to lop in the 
‘Il-treated forests, as a punishment and as a means of allowing the treos to recover, or 
he might fix a lopping rotation of three years for any given forest, opening and closing 
a compartment a year, which is the only possible way of enforcing rule No. 3 as given 
in parngraph 80. In these old oaks it is often dinicult to determine which is the leading 
shoot, as it frequently happons that one shoot is left as the leader one year, bul is cut 
down tho next und another left. The rule should therefore run ; ‘© The leading shoot 
shall not be lopped and no branch out within four feet of the tup of the tree,” 


The special rules for Kulsi are menticned in paragraph 171. 

The villuyers of Lohari, khat Dhanau, are anxious to obtain permission to lop 
the oaks in BodiAr, and ns these are rather too remote from Chukrata to be of great 
value as fucl, they might be allowed to do eo, as avon as the ranger reports that the 
lopping rules have been duly obgerved in the II, class forests of their khat, and 


( 92 ) 


on the distinct understanding that the permission will be at once withdrawn in caso 
the rules are agnin disregarded. 


188. (5) Hemoving dead leaves.—It would be extremely difficult to check tho 
number of “ kandia” full of dead leaves removed from a forest, besides giving the 
forest guard an opportunity for oppression. The hest plan, therefore, at Kurwa (vide 
paragraph 3)) would be to adopt the second alternative and to open a portion of the 
forest annually for this purpose.

ment of rent to the Forest Departmentto break up any grassy blinks in the open 
11. class forests which contain no forest growth, will not be planted up, and are not 
likely to get stocked with natural seedlings, but no clearances of forest growth should 
ou any account be allowed.

MEASUREMENT OF GROWING OAKS. 


191. Tho measurements should be made regularly once a year in the sample 
plot of kurshu in Jidi 9, but the present sample plota of mora and bén should be 
abandoned ns being unsuitable for the purpose, cide para. 118, and fresh ones chosen 
of one acre in extent : that for bin in one of the closed Cantonment blocks, that for 
moru in Matka@ugra 2. ‘The practice of giving the results of the measurement of 12 
average trees in the unnual report should bo discontinued as usoless, and cithor the 
average increment for each diametor class recorded, or simply a note made to the 
effect that the plots were measured on_a certain date and the measurements entered 
in the Forest Journal, 

CouNnTING CONCENTRIO RINGS IN OONIFERS. 


192. This should be continued for deodir, rai, and morinda, some kail also 
being counted when possible, as up to date no ring countings have been made to 
show the rate of growth of this species, probably the fastest growing conifer in the 
range. The record should be kept by compartments and both the aspect and altitude 
should be invariably noted. The rings of at least fifty trees in each block should be 
counted at the time when the fellinga take place, and all irregularly shaped trees 
should be rejected, as it is not easy in such cuses to choose a mean radius, whilst uny 
trees which seem to have boon suppressed should bear a special mark to distinguish 
them from the others. The record should be kept in tho Forest Journal in a similar 
form to that of Appendix P. with the addition of a coluimn for altitudes, 


REDUGING FACTOR. 


193. The reducing factor for spruce and silver fir seems to have been fairly 
well determined for 1. and IL. clays trees, and no further measurements need be made ; 
but it is necessary to find out the reducing factors of the three oaks, and as a consi- 
derable number of 111. class trees are felled in addition to the larger ones, trees of 
each species and of each of tue three diameter classes 12” —18,” 18% — 24” and over 
24” should be selected and their mean reducing factor determined according to the 


method given in para, 119, 
MAINTENANCE OF I?ECORDS. 
194. Skeleton mnps of the different working circles should be prepared and at 
the end of each year the orea over which selection or other fellings have been made 


should be shown by means of different colors and the quantity of produce removed 
written down iu a column at the side. Most of the compartments are well detined by 


eae: 


natural features. I consider also that skeleton maps showing the bonndaries, the 
chaks, all pillars, the divisions into blocks and compartments, the principal roada, &c., 
with names in the vernacolar but numbers in Knglish, should be prepared for thé 
use of foresters and forest guards, and the former should be held responsible that 
their guards are acquainted with the proper boundaries of the blocks and compart- 
ments in their beats. Both foreaters and guards are often ignorant of the divisions 
which have been formed, and in their reports frequently mention local names 
which are unknown to the divisional officer, thus rendering the maintenance of proper 
records impossible. All guards should bo expected to know Kuglish pumbers, since 
the pillars, stacks of fuol, and so forth ure all numbered in English. 


In fuel fellings trees of very various sizes are out, this being especially the case 
with onks owing to the number of unsound ones of all classes. It is therefore useless, 
not to say misleading, simply to give the total number of treas felled as in para. 48 
of the annual report for 1887, but a record should be carefully kept of the number 
of trees of the different dinmeter classes cut down, distingnishing between the sound 
and the unsound as has been done in the enumeration survey, vide Appendix A. 


Hitherto coppicing has only been carried on at Kurwa, and it seems to have 
been the custom to count all the trees felled and enter them as thinning, vida the para. 
referred to above. This is useless and involves a great deal of unnecessary trouble, 
What ehould be recorded in coppice operations is simply the number of acres coppiced 
und the number of etandards left of difterent diameter classes with the names of the 
species.

APPENDIX @. 


Join? Memorandum on Forest Rights in Jaunsdr Bawar by Mr. Ross, C.S., Mr. Barre, C.8., and 
Ma. Fisugr, Officiating Conservator of Forests, dated January, 1885. 


1. In socond class forests the villagers may gonerally cut their annual allotmont of inferior trees accord- 
ing to list givon In the record-of-righta, whoro and when they like; but in thirteen khats near Chakrita, a list 
of which is given in tho Appendix, where the maintonance of a steady fuel supply for the troops is of the first 
importance, all right-holdors’ trees shall be marked in October by the Divisional Forost Oficcr, or by his orders, 
and removed by the villagera whenever they ike, within a period of twelve months. In case eny marked trecs 
have not byen removed within this period, they will be counted again towards the next year’s annual grant. 


3. For right-holders, deodiir, pines, and firs, which may be claawed aa valuable timber, will bo marked in 
October in all the khats by the Divisional Forust Officers, or by his orders. No unmarked and valuable timber 
nay be removed by « right-holder 


3. All deodir trees given to right-holdors must be sawn up and not cut into planks with tho exo, 


4. Villagers have no rights whatover in first-class reserves, and at present there is an ample supply of 
deoddr in second-class reserves, where the deuddr trees for the right-holders will bo marked exclusively. It 
will be tho duty of the Forest: Department to work the second-class reserves in such a way that tho annual 
supply of right-holders’ deodiir inay be maintained. 


5. Major Bailey’s memorandum on the forest rights in Jaunsir Biwar, dated 20th February, 1884, con- 


tains @ concise and accurate account of these rights, which have also been noted in the record-of-righta in 
each khat. 


6. In consequence of the large area of second eluss forvst, which has been closed in the vicinity of Chak- 
rita, it is expedient that, in future, no further area should be closed unless « corresponding aren is oponod, in 


which reproduction has boen effected, and in which the young growth is no longer in danger of serious injury by 
cattle and sheop. 


In order that the villagers may not be unduly inconvenioneed hy this practice, the consant of the Superin- 
tendent of the Diin shoul! be obtained, aud any caso of differonce of opiuion botween the Conservator of Forests 
and the Superintendent of the Dén should be referred for the decision of Government. 


List showing tho khats in Jaunsér Béwar near Chakrdta Cantonment ; ~ 


1. Dissau. 6. Bislir. 9. Bangion. 

2. Dhanau. 6. Dwar. 10. Bamtar. 

8. Kailo. 7. Mobna. 11. Sell. 

4. Lakhau. 8. Barasua. 12, Koru.

APPENDIX 0. 


Agreement of Sth of May, 1877, between the Commissariat and Forest Departments for the supply of swood 
Jor ust of the troops at Ohakrdia, sanctioned by the Commissary-General in Ms No. 22) of 22nd May, 


1877. 


1. An indent to be furnished hy the Commissariat in Fobruary of cach year, showing the reqmroments 
monthly during the ensuing year and an estimate of the probable lump domand for the year following 

2. Word to be made over monthly to the Comminsariat from April to Novenbor and bi-monthly from 
December ta March, 

8. Transfor receipta for wood taken over during tha month to be eubmitted by the 10th of the following 
month 

4 Wood to be made over at the Forest Depét, Morrow’s Neck, and at a oconiral depat in Canton. 
ments. 

5. The rate for wood to be fixed annually in March of each year, the rate for 1877-78 being Ra 14-1 
per 100 gubic foet of fir and Ra 19-1 per 100 cuble foot of oak, or Ha. 45 per 100 maunds of fir and Ry 45-12 
per 100 maunds of oak. 

6. Wood to be inado ovor by measurement, and the prosent moasurementa of 320 cubic foot of fir and 
240 cubis fect of onk as equivalent to 100 maunds to continue until a final figure be arrived at with consent of 
both Departments, it being understood that, for tho presont, tho Commissariat will credit to the Forust Depart- 
went any surplus, and on the other hand the Forest Department wil] make ood any deficiencies, 

7. Wood to be taken over in lengths averaging 2 foet, the Commissariat recruiting into shorter longtha 
as may be necessary 

8 Tho Commissariat to give one year's natice from March in case they should wish to make other 
arrangements for the supply of wood, and almilurly the Forest Depirtment, should they wish to couse the sup. 
plies. 

9 +A shed In cantonmenta to be Imilt by the Forest Dopartment at an approximate cost of Kx 5,000 ; this 
amount t) be recovered from the Commissariat Departient by an increase of the charge on the fuel spreud over 
a period of four years from the lat of April, 1878 

10 The shed to be considerod aa belonging conjointly to hoth Departmenia. 

11) The wood in tho shed to he considered os bolonging to the Forest Depirtment until taken over by the 
Commussarat, provided only that when ouce placed m the uhed, it ia to be ysod only for supply to the Com- 
nissuriat: Department 

12 Wood stacked in the shed to bo used during the raina, my July, August, and September, and again un 
the winter months, January, Fobruary, and March ; during the other months from open forest depdtx 

13. The shed to be filled by the Forest Department during April and May and again in October ana 
November. 

14 The shed to contain about 50,000 cube fect of wood, or approximately 17,000 maunda

lency the Governor-General in Council, copy of a Resolution

No. 823 F or 1888. 


&Gs—6 
RESOLUTION, 
REVENUE (Forests) DerARTMENT. 
Dated Allahabad, the 24th December, 1888. 


Reap— 
(J) The Working Plan of the Deoban Range, Jaunsfr Forest Division 


(2) Letter from the Inspecter-General of Foresta to the Government of India, No. 845, dated 

6th November, 1888, 

OsstrvaTions.—The working plan cited in the papers read 
above is for the forests of the Deoban range in Jaunsir Biwar adjoin- 
ing the cantonment of Chakrata. The area comprised by thesc forests 
is only 46 square miles, of which thrce square miles are first class 
forests and 43 second class. The so-called ‘second class" forests 
are forests in which, though they have been declared “ reserved” 
under the Forest Act, the neighbouring villages have been assigned 
extensive privileges which are helieved to be essential to their well 
being, and which were taken into account and recorded at the last 
land-revenue settlement. Mr. Ilearle, the author of the plan, has 
fully doscribed the history and nature of these privileges, and has 
indicated the directions in which, from a Forest Officer's point of view, 
their further curtailment or regulation is ultimately desirable. This 
portion of the plan will be of much use to the District Officers of the 
pargana, a3 it summarises in a convenient form the arrangements which 
have been made in respect of these forests from time totime. But 
His Honor desires it to be distinctly understood that the general sanc- 
tion which is now given to the working plan does not impair or alter 
the settlement of village grazing and other rights in the second class 

“GO No saF.datea orests effected by the rules* of 1885. The closure 
Ae ees of certain areas against grazing, for example, which 
Mr. Hearle recommends, will require, under Rule 6G, the consent of the 
Superintendent of the Din. The rule regarding the compulsory use 
of the saw, and the fine of two annas for each breach of the rule, which 
are mentioned in paragraphs 28 and 184, have been cancelled by G. O. 
No. We dated the Gth September, 1888. The question of limiting the 
number of cattle, goats, and sheep entitled to free grazing in the second 
class forests is in train of scttlement. 


2. The portion of the plan which treats of the proposed lvase 
by the Forest Department of an area of 1,585 acres of oak forest from 
the Cantonment Committee of Chakrata, and sketches the method 
on which this area would be worked for the fuel requirements of the 
cantonment, has been referred for consideration to the Cantonment 
Committee. It will rest with the Military authoritics to determine 
whether the lease should be granted or not. Should they not wish to 
accept the propoval of the Forest Department, the programme of work 
suggested by Mr. Hearle may still prove useful to the Cantonment 
Committee in the management of these lands. 


( 2) 


3. The general object of the plan is to supply timber and fuel to 
Chakrita, and Jlis Honor sees no reason for doubting the opinion ex- 
pressed by the Inspector-General of Forests that the actual and prospec- 
tive demands of the cantonments can be fully met fgom the first and 
second class forests under the control of the Department in Jaunsir- 
Biwar, Mr. earle's proposals have been professionally approved, 
and the working plan is now provisionally sanctioned by Govern- 
ment for a term of 10 years. Sir Auckland Colvin, fowever, entirely 
agrees with the Inspector-General of Forests that so elaborate and 
costly a work was unnecessary, and that a simple plan of operations 
for the next 10 or 20 years would have fully met the requirements 
of the case. The over-elaboration of the working plans prepared in 
the School Circle during the last few years has repeatedly been 
remarked upon by this Government, and Sir Auckland Colvin is glad 
to find that the same viow is accepted by the head of the Forest 
Department, who acts as the professional adviser to Provincial Govern- 
ments in all matters teuching the character and details of forest 
working plans. Asno working plans of a similarly comprehensive 
character are now under preparation, it is unnecessary to dwell fur- 
ther upon the waste of time and money which are entailed by such 
necdlessly elaborate compilations. But Sir Auckland Colvin would 
place on record his regret that sufficient attention should not have been 
paid in this and other cases to practical requirements, and to consider- 
ations of.economy which ought to regulate the procedure of all depart- 
ments of the administration. 


T. W. HOLDERNESS, 
Secy. to Govt., N.-W. P. and Oudh.
